The Pelletier–Ressayre hidden symmetry for Littlewood–Richardson coefficients
Darij Grinberg

Abstract
Abstract. We prove an identity for Littlewood–Richardson coefficients conjectured by Pelletier and Ressayre. The proof relies on a novel birational involution defined over any semifield. Keywords: symmetric functions, Schur functions, Schur polynomials, Littlewood–Richardson coefficients, birational combinatorics, detropicalization, partitions. MSC2010 Mathematics Subject Classifications: 05E05.



***

One of the central concepts in the theory of symmetric functions are the

Littlewood–Richardson coefficients

c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
: the
coefficients when a product
s_{\mu}s_{\nu}
of two Schur functions is
expanded back in the Schur basis
\left(s_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}
. Various properties of these coefficients have been
found, among them combinatorial interpretations, vanishing results, bounds and
symmetries (i.e., equalities between
c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
for different

\lambda,\mu,\nu
). A recent overview of the latter can be found in

[
BriRos20
]
.

In
[
PelRes20
]
, Pelletier and Ressayre conjectured a further symmetry of
Littlewood–Richardson coefficients. Unless the classical ones, it is a
partial symmetry (i.e., it does not cover every Littlewood–Richardson
coefficient); it is furthermore much less simple to state, to the extent that
Pelletier and Ressayre have conjectured its existence while leaving open the
question which exact coefficients it matches up. In this paper, we answer this
question and prove the conjecture thus concretized.

The conjecture, in its original (unconcrete) form, can be stated as follows:
Let
n\geq 2
, and consider the set
\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
of
all partitions having length
\leq n
. Let
a
and
b
be two nonnegative
integers, and define the two partitions
\alpha=\left(a+b,a^{n-2}\right)

and
\beta=\left(a+b,b^{n-2}\right)
(where
c^{n-2}
means

\underbrace{c,c,\ldots,c}_{n-2\text{ times}}
, as usual in partition
combinatorics). Fix another partition
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
. Then, the families
\left(c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}\right)_{\omega\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}
and
\left(c_{\beta,\mu}^{\omega}\right)_{\omega\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}
of
Littlewood–Richardson coefficients seem to be identical up to permutation.
(We can restate this in terms of Schur polynomials in the
n
variables

x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
; this then becomes the claim that the products

s_{\alpha}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
and
s_{\beta}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
,
when expanded in the basis of Schur polynomials, have the same multiset of coefficients.)

Pelletier and Ressayre have proved this conjecture for
n=3
(see

[
PelRes20
, Corollary 2]
) and in some further cases. We shall prove it in
full generality, and construct what is essentially a bijection
\varphi:\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]\rightarrow\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
that makes it explicit (i.e., that satisfies
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\varphi\left(\omega\right)}
for each
\omega\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
). To be fully precise,
\varphi

will not be a bijection
\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]\rightarrow\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
, but rather a bijection from

\mathbb{Z}^{n}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and it will satisfy
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\varphi\left(\omega\right)}
with the
understanding that
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\omega}=0
when

\omega\notin\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
. (Here,

\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
is understood to be a subset of

\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by identifying each partition
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
with the
n
-tuple
\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)
.)

We will define this bijection
\varphi
by explicit (if somewhat intricate)
formulas that “mingle” the entries of the
partition it is being applied to with those of
\mu
(as well as
a
and
b
)
using the
\min
and
+
operators. These formulas are best understood in the

birational picture
, in which these
\min
and
+
operators are
generalized to the addition and the multiplication of an arbitrary semifield.
(Our proof does not require this generality, but the birational picture has
the advantage of greater familiarity and better notational support. It also
reveals a connection with a known birational map known as a “birational R-matrix” (see Section
5.4
for
details), which throws some light on the otherwise rather mysterious bijection.)

Another ingredient of our proof is an explicit formula for
s_{\alpha}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
for the above-mentioned partition
\alpha
.

Remark on alternative versions

This paper also has a detailed version
[
Grinbe20
]
, which includes some
proofs that have been omitted from the present version (mostly basic
properties of symmetric functions).

An older version of this paper appeared as

Oberwolfach Preprint
OWP-2020-18
.

1
Notations

We will use the following notations (most of which are also used in

[
GriRei20
, §2.1]
):

•

We let
\mathbb{N}=\left\{0,1,2,\ldots\right\}
.

•

We fix a commutative ring
\mathbf{k}
; we will use this
\mathbf{k}
as
the base ring in what follows.

•

A
weak composition
means an infinite sequence of nonnegative
integers that contains only finitely many nonzero entries (i.e., a sequence

\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
such that all but finitely many
i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
satisfy
\alpha_{i}=0
).

•

We let
\operatorname*{WC}
denote the set of all weak compositions.

•

For any weak composition
\alpha
and any positive integer
i
, we let

\alpha_{i}
denote the
i
-th entry of
\alpha
(so that
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right)
). More generally, we use
this notation whenever
\alpha
is an infinite sequence of any kind of objects.

•

The
size

\left|\alpha\right|
of a weak composition

\alpha
is defined to be
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\cdots\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

A
partition
means a weak composition whose entries weakly
decrease (i.e., a weak composition
\alpha
satisfying
\alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{2}\geq\alpha_{3}\geq\cdots
).

•

We let
\operatorname*{Par}
denote the set of all partitions.

•

We will sometimes omit trailing zeroes from partitions: i.e., a
partition
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots\right)
will be identified with the
k
-tuple
\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\right)
whenever
k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\lambda_{k+1}=\lambda_{k+2}=\lambda_{k+3}=\cdots=0
. For example,
\left(3,2,1,0,0,0,\ldots\right)=\left(3,2,1\right)=\left(3,2,1,0\right)
.

As a consequence of this, an
n
-tuple
\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(for any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
) is a partition if and only if it satisfies
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 0
.

•

A
part
of a partition
\lambda
means a nonzero entry of

\lambda
. For example, the parts of the partition
\left(3,1,1\right)=\left(3,1,1,0,0,0,\ldots\right)
are
3,1,1
.

•

The
length
of a partition
\lambda
means the smallest

k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\lambda_{k+1}=\lambda_{k+2}=\lambda_{k+3}=\cdots=0
. Equivalently, the length of a partition
\lambda
is the number of
parts of
\lambda
(counted with multiplicity). This length is denoted by

\ell\left(\lambda\right)
. For example,
\ell\left(\left(4,2,0,0\right)\right)=\ell\left(\left(4,2\right)\right)=2
and

\ell\left(\left(5,1,1\right)\right)=3
.

•

We will use the notation
m^{k}
for “
\underbrace{m,m,\ldots,m}_{k\text{ times}}
” in partitions
and tuples (whenever
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
). (For example,

\left(2,1^{4}\right)=\left(2,1,1,1,1\right)
.)

•

We let
\Lambda
denote the ring of symmetric functions in infinitely
many variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
over
\mathbf{k}
. This is a subring
of the ring
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
of formal power series. To be more specific,
\Lambda
consists of
all power series in
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
that are symmetric (i.e., invariant under
permutations of the variables) and of bounded degree (see
[
GriRei20
, §2.1]
for the precise meaning of this).

•

A
monomial
shall mean a formal expression of the form

x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}x_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots
with
\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}
. Formal power series are formal infinite
\mathbf{k}
-linear combinations of such monomials.

•

For any weak composition
\alpha
, we let
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}
denote
the monomial
x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}x_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots
.

•

The
degree
of a monomial
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}
is defined to be

\left|\alpha\right|
.

We shall use the symmetric functions
h_{n}
and
s_{\lambda}
in
\Lambda
as
defined in
[
GriRei20
, Sections 2.1 and 2.2]
. Let us briefly recall how they
are defined:

•

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the
complete homogeneous
symmetric function
h_{n}\in\Lambda
by

h_{n}=\sum\limits_{i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{n}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{n}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}.

Thus,
h_{0}=1
and
h_{n}=0
for all
n<0
.

•

For each partition
\lambda
, we define the
Schur function

s_{\lambda}\in\Lambda
by

s_{\lambda}=\sum\limits\mathbf{x}_{T},

where the sum ranges over all semistandard tableaux
T
of shape
\lambda
,
and where
\mathbf{x}_{T}
denotes the monomial obtained by multiplying the

x_{i}
for all entries
i
of
T
. We refer the reader to
[
GriRei20
, Definition
2.2.1]
or to
[
Stanle01
, §7.10]
for the details of this
definition and further descriptions of the Schur functions.

The family
\left(s_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}
is a
basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\Lambda
, and is known as the
Schur
basis
. It is easy to see that each
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies
s_{\left(n\right)}=h_{n}
.

•

We shall use the
Littlewood–Richardson coefficients
c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
(for
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\operatorname*{Par}
), as defined in

[
GriRei20
, Definition 2.5.8]
, in
[
Stanle01
, §7.15]
or in

[
Egge19
, Chapter 10]
. One of their defining properties is the following
fact (see, e.g.,
[
GriRei20
, (2.5.6)]
or
[
Stanle01
, (7.64)]
or

[
Egge19
, (10.1)]
): Any two partitions
\mu,\nu\in\operatorname*{Par}

satisfy

s_{\mu}s_{\nu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}s_{\lambda}.

(1)

2
The theorem

Convention 2.1
.

(a)

For the rest of this paper, we fix a positive integer
n
.

(b)

Let
\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
be the set of
all partitions having length
\leq n
. In other words,

\displaystyle\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]

\displaystyle=\left\{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\ \mid\ \lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)\right\}=\operatorname*{Par}\cap\mathbb{N}^{n}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\ \mid\ \lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 0\right\}

(where we are using our convention that trailing zeroes can be omitted from
partitions, so that a partition of length
\leq n
can always be identified
with an
n
-tuple).

(c)

A family
\left(u_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
objects (e.g., of numbers) is said to be
n
-periodic
if each

j\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
u_{j}=u_{j+n}
. Equivalently, a family
\left(u_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of objects is
n
-periodic if and only if it
has the property that

\left(u_{j}=u_{j^{\prime}}\text{ whenever }j\text{ and }j^{\prime}\text{ are
two integers satisfying }j\equiv j^{\prime}\operatorname{mod}n\right).

Thus, an
n
-periodic family
\left(u_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
uniquely determined by the
n
entries
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n}
(because for
any integer
j
, we have
u_{j}=u_{j^{\prime}}
, where
j^{\prime}
is the
unique element of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
that is congruent to
j

modulo
n
).

Example 2.1
.

If
n=3
, then both partitions
\left(3,2\right)
and
\left(3,2,2\right)
belong to
\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
, while the
partition
\left(3,2,2,2\right)
does not. The
n
-tuples
\left(4,2,1\right)
and
\left(3,3,0\right)
are partitions, while the

n
-tuples
\left(1,0,-1\right)
and
\left(2,0,1\right)
are not.

If
\zeta
is an
n
-th root of unity, then the family
\left(\zeta^{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of complex numbers is
n
-periodic.

We can now state our main theorem, which is a concretization of

[
PelRes20
, Conjecture 1]
:

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume that
n\geq 2
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
.

Define the two partitions
\alpha=\left(a+b,a^{n-2}\right)
and

\beta=\left(a+b,b^{n-2}\right)
.

Fix any partition
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.

Define a map
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as follows:

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Define an
n
-tuple
\nu=\left(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by

\nu_{i}=\omega_{i}-a\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\},

where
\omega_{i}
means the
i
-th entry of
\omega
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
i\#
denote the unique element of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
congruent to
i
modulo
n
.

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

\displaystyle\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\left(\nu_{\left(j+1\right)\#}+\nu_{\left(j+2\right)\#}+\cdots+\nu_{\left(j+k\right)\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.+\left(\mu_{\left(j+k+1\right)\#}+\mu_{\left(j+k+2\right)\#}+\cdots+\mu_{\left(j+n-1\right)\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}\vphantom{\left(\nu_{\left(j+1\right)\#}\right)}\right\}.

Define an
n
-tuple
\eta=\left(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by setting

\eta_{i}=\mu_{i\#}+\left(\mu_{\left(i-1\right)\#}+\tau_{\left(i-1\right)\#}\right)-\left(\nu_{\left(i+1\right)\#}+\tau_{\left(i+1\right)\#}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Let
\varphi\left(\omega\right)
be the
n
-tuple
\left(\eta_{1}+b,\eta_{2}+b,\ldots,\eta_{n}+b\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Thus, we have
defined a map
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Then:

(a)

The map
\varphi
is a bijection.

(b)

We have

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\varphi\left(\omega\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Here, we are using the convention that every
n
-tuple
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
that is not a partition satisfies
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=0

and
c_{\beta,\mu}^{\omega}=0
.

This theorem will be proved at the end of this paper, after we have shown
several (often seemingly unrelated, yet eventually useful) results.

Example 2.3
.

Let
n=4
and
a=1
and
b=4
. The partitions
\alpha
and
\beta
defined in
Theorem
2.2
then take the forms
\alpha=\left(1+4,1^{2}\right)=\left(5,1,1\right)
and
\beta=\left(1+4,4^{2}\right)=\left(5,4,4\right)
.

Let
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
be the partition
\left(2,1\right)=\left(2,1,0,0\right)
. Let
\omega\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
be the partition
\left(5,3,2\right)=\left(5,3,2,0\right)
. We shall compute the
n
-tuple
\varphi\left(\omega\right)
defined in Theorem
2.2
.

Indeed, the
n
-tuple
\nu
from Theorem
2.2
is

\nu=\left(\omega_{1}-a,\omega_{2}-a,\omega_{3}-a,\omega_{4}-a\right)=\left(5-1,3-1,2-1,0-1\right)=\left(4,2,1,-1\right).

The integers
i\#
from Theorem
2.2
form an
n
-periodic family

\left(i\#\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}=\left(\ldots,0\#,1\#,2\#,3\#,4\#,5\#,6\#,7\#,\ldots\right)=\left(\ldots,4,1,2,3,4,1,2,3,\ldots\right).

The integers
\tau_{j}
(for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
) from Theorem
2.2
are
given by

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\left(\nu_{2\#}+\nu_{3\#}+\cdots+\nu_{\left(k+1\right)\#}\right)+\left(\mu_{\left(k+2\right)\#}+\mu_{\left(k+3\right)\#}+\cdots+\mu_{4\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,2,3\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{2\#}+\mu_{3\#}+\mu_{4\#},\ \ \ \nu_{2\#}+\mu_{3\#}+\mu_{4\#},\ \ \ \nu_{2\#}+\nu_{3\#}+\mu_{4\#},\ \ \ \nu_{2\#}+\nu_{3\#}+\nu_{4\#}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}+\mu_{4},\ \ \ \nu_{2}+\mu_{3}+\mu_{4},\ \ \ \nu_{2}+\nu_{3}+\mu_{4},\ \ \ \nu_{2}+\nu_{3}+\nu_{4}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{1+0+0,\ \ \ 2+0+0,\ \ \ 2+1+0,\ \ \ 2+1+\left(-1\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{1,2,3,2\right\}=1

and

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\left(\nu_{3\#}+\nu_{4\#}+\cdots+\nu_{\left(k+2\right)\#}\right)+\left(\mu_{\left(k+3\right)\#}+\mu_{\left(k+4\right)\#}+\cdots+\mu_{5\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,2,3\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{3\#}+\mu_{4\#}+\mu_{5\#},\ \ \ \nu_{3\#}+\mu_{4\#}+\mu_{5\#},\ \ \ \nu_{3\#}+\nu_{4\#}+\mu_{5\#},\ \ \ \nu_{3\#}+\nu_{4\#}+\nu_{5\#}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{3}+\mu_{4}+\mu_{1},\ \ \ \nu_{3}+\mu_{4}+\mu_{1},\ \ \ \nu_{3}+\nu_{4}+\mu_{1},\ \ \ \nu_{3}+\nu_{4}+\nu_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{0+0+2,\ \ \ 1+0+2,\ \ \ 1+\left(-1\right)+2,\ \ \ 1+\left(-1\right)+4\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{2,3,2,4\right\}=2

and

\displaystyle\tau_{3}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\left(\nu_{4\#}+\nu_{5\#}+\cdots+\nu_{\left(k+3\right)\#}\right)+\left(\mu_{\left(k+4\right)\#}+\mu_{\left(k+5\right)\#}+\cdots+\mu_{6\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,2,3\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{4\#}+\mu_{5\#}+\mu_{6\#},\ \ \ \nu_{4\#}+\mu_{5\#}+\mu_{6\#},\ \ \ \nu_{4\#}+\nu_{5\#}+\mu_{6\#},\ \ \ \nu_{4\#}+\nu_{5\#}+\nu_{6\#}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{4}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\ \ \ \nu_{4}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\ \ \ \nu_{4}+\nu_{1}+\mu_{2},\ \ \ \nu_{4}+\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{0+2+1,\ \ \ \left(-1\right)+2+1,\ \ \ \left(-1\right)+4+1,\ \ \ \left(-1\right)+4+2\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{3,2,4,5\right\}=2

and

\displaystyle\tau_{4}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\left(\nu_{5\#}+\nu_{6\#}+\cdots+\nu_{\left(k+4\right)\#}\right)+\left(\mu_{\left(k+5\right)\#}+\mu_{\left(k+6\right)\#}+\cdots+\mu_{7\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,2,3\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{5\#}+\mu_{6\#}+\mu_{7\#},\ \ \ \nu_{5\#}+\mu_{6\#}+\mu_{7\#},\ \ \ \nu_{5\#}+\nu_{6\#}+\mu_{7\#},\ \ \ \nu_{5\#}+\nu_{6\#}+\nu_{7\#}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3},\ \ \ \nu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3},\ \ \ \nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+\mu_{3},\ \ \ \nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+\nu_{3}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{2+1+0,\ \ \ 4+1+0,\ \ \ 4+2+0,\ \ \ 4+2+1\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{3,5,6,7\right\}=3

and

\tau_{j}=\tau_{j^{\prime}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{whenever }j\equiv j^{\prime}\operatorname{mod}4

(the latter equality follows from the
n
-periodicity of the family
\left(i\#\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
). Thus, the
n
-tuple
\eta=\left(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{n}\right)
from Theorem
2.2
is given
by

\eta_{1}=\underbrace{\mu_{1\#}}_{=\mu_{1}=2}+\left(\underbrace{\mu_{0\#}}_{=\mu_{4}=0}+\underbrace{\tau_{0\#}}_{=\tau_{4}=3}\right)-\left(\underbrace{\nu_{2\#}}_{=\nu_{2}=2}+\underbrace{\tau_{2\#}}_{=\tau_{2}=2}\right)=2+\left(0+3\right)-\left(2+2\right)=1

and

\eta_{2}=\underbrace{\mu_{2\#}}_{=\mu_{2}=1}+\left(\underbrace{\mu_{1\#}}_{=\mu_{1}=2}+\underbrace{\tau_{1\#}}_{=\tau_{1}=1}\right)-\left(\underbrace{\nu_{3\#}}_{=\nu_{3}=1}+\underbrace{\tau_{3\#}}_{=\tau_{3}=2}\right)=1+\left(2+1\right)-\left(1+2\right)=1

and

\eta_{3}=\underbrace{\mu_{3\#}}_{=\mu_{3}=0}+\left(\underbrace{\mu_{2\#}}_{=\mu_{2}=1}+\underbrace{\tau_{2\#}}_{=\tau_{2}=2}\right)-\left(\underbrace{\nu_{4\#}}_{=\nu_{4}=-1}+\underbrace{\tau_{4\#}}_{=\tau_{4}=3}\right)=0+\left(1+2\right)-\left(\left(-1\right)+3\right)=1

and

\eta_{4}=\underbrace{\mu_{4\#}}_{=\mu_{4}=0}+\left(\underbrace{\mu_{3\#}}_{=\mu_{3}=0}+\underbrace{\tau_{3\#}}_{=\tau_{3}=2}\right)-\left(\underbrace{\nu_{5\#}}_{=\nu_{1}=4}+\underbrace{\tau_{5\#}}_{=\tau_{1}=1}\right)=0+\left(0+2\right)-\left(4+1\right)=-3,

so
\eta=\left(1,1,1,-3\right)
. Hence,
\varphi\left(\omega\right)=\left(1+b,1+b,1+b,-3+b\right)=\left(5,5,5,1\right)
(since
b=4
).
This is a partition. Theorem
2.2

(b)
now yields

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\varphi\left(\omega\right)}
, that
is,
c_{\left(5,1,1\right),\left(2,1\right)}^{\left(5,3,2\right)}=c_{\left(5,4,4\right),\left(2,1\right)}^{\left(5,5,5,1\right)}
.
And indeed, this equality holds (both of its sides being equal to
1
).

Question 2.4
.

Can the bijection
\varphi
in Theorem
2.2
be defined in a more
“intuitive” way, similar to (e.g.)
jeu-de-taquin or the RSK correspondence? (Of course, there is no tableau being
transformed here, just a partition, but this should make this construction easier.)

3
A birational involution

The leading role in our proof of Theorem
2.2
will be played by a
certain piecewise-linear involution (which is similar to the bijection

\varphi
in Theorem
2.2
, but without the shifting by
-a
and

b
). For the sake of convenience, we prefer to study this involution in a
more general setting, in which the operations
\min
,
+
and
-
are replaced
by the structure operations
+
,
\cdot
and
/
of a semifield. This kind of
generalization is called
detropicalization
(or
birational
lifting
, or
tropicalization
in the older combinatorial literature);
see, e.g.,
[
Kirill01
]
,
[
NouYam02
]
,
[
EinPro13
, Sections 5 and
9]
or
[
Roby15
, §4.2]
for examples of this procedure (although
our use of it will be conceptually simpler).

3.1
Semifields

We recall some basic definitions from basic abstract algebra (mostly to avoid
confusion arising from slight terminological differences):

•

A
semigroup
means a pair
\left(S,\ast\right)
, where
S
is
a set and where
\ast
is an associative binary operation on
S
. We do not
require this operation
\ast
to have a neutral element. We usually write the
operation
\ast
infix (i.e., we write
a\ast b
instead of
\ast\left(a,b\right)
when
a,b\in S
).

•

A semigroup
\left(S,\ast\right)
is said to be
abelian
if
the operation
\ast
is commutative (i.e., we have
a\ast b=b\ast a
for all

a,b\in S
).

•

A
monoid
means a triple
\left(S,\ast,e\right)
, where

\left(S,\ast\right)
is a semigroup and where
e
is a neutral element
for the operation
\ast
(that is,
e
is an element of
S
that satisfies

e\ast a=a\ast e=a
for each
a\in S
). Usually, the monoid
\left(S,\ast,e\right)
is equated with the semigroup
\left(S,\ast\right)

because the neutral element is uniquely determined by
S
and
\ast
.

•

If
\left(S,\ast,e\right)
is a monoid and
a
is an element of
S
,
then an
inverse
of
a
(with respect to
\ast
) means an element
b
of

S
satisfying
a\ast b=b\ast a=e
. Such an inverse of
a
is always unique
when it exists.

•

A
group
means a monoid
\left(S,\ast,e\right)
such that each
element of
S
has an inverse (with respect to
\ast
).

We next recall the definition of a semifield (more precisely, the one we will
be using, as there are many competing ones):

Definition 3.1
.

A
semifield
means a set
\mathbb{K}
endowed with

•

two binary operations called “addition” and “multiplication”, and denoted by
+
and
\cdot
,
respectively, and both written infix (i.e., we write
a+b
and
a\cdot b

instead of
+\left(a,b\right)
and
\cdot\left(a,b\right)
), and

•

an element called “unity” and denoted
by
1

such that
\left(\mathbb{K},+\right)
is an abelian semigroup
and
\left(\mathbb{K},\cdot,1\right)
is an abelian group, and such that
the following axiom is satisfied:

•

Distributivity:
We have
a\cdot\left(b+c\right)=\left(a\cdot b\right)+\left(a\cdot c\right)
and
\left(a+b\right)\cdot c=\left(a\cdot c\right)+\left(b\cdot c\right)
for all
a\in\mathbb{K}
,
b\in\mathbb{K}
and
c\in\mathbb{K}
.

Thus, a semifield is similar to a field, except that it has no additive
inverses and no zero element, but, on the other hand, has multiplicative
inverses for all its elements (not just the nonzero ones).

Example 3.2
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}_{+}
be the set of all positive
rational numbers. Then,
\mathbb{Q}_{+}
(endowed with its standard addition
and multiplication and the number
1
) is a semifield.

Example 3.3
.

Let
\left(\mathbb{A},\ast,e\right)
be any
totally ordered abelian group (whose operation is
\ast
and whose neutral
element is
e
). Then,
\mathbb{A}
becomes a semifield if we endow it with
the “addition”
\min
(that is, we set

a+b:=\min\left\{a,b\right\}
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{A}
), the
“multiplication”
\ast
(that is, we set

a\cdot b:=a\ast b
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{A}
), and the “unity”
e
. This semifield
\left(\mathbb{A},\min,\ast,e\right)
is called the
min tropical semifield
of
\left(\mathbb{A},\ast,e\right)
.

Convention 3.1
.

All conventions that are typically used for
fields will be used for semifields as well, to the extent they apply. Specifically:

•

If
\mathbb{K}
is a semifield, and if
a,b\in\mathbb{K}
, then
a\cdot b
shall be abbreviated by
ab
.

•

We shall use the standard “PEMDAS” convention that multiplication-like operations have higher precedence than
addition-like operations; thus, e.g., the expression “
ab+ac
” must be understood as “
\left(ab\right)+\left(ac\right)
” (and not, for example, as
“
a\left(b+a\right)c
”).

•

If
\mathbb{K}
is a semifield, then the inverse of any element

b\in\mathbb{K}
in the abelian group
\left(\mathbb{K},\cdot,1\right)

will be denoted by
b^{-1}
. Note that this inverse is always defined (unlike
when
\mathbb{K}
is a field).

•

If
\mathbb{K}
is a semifield, and if
a,b\in\mathbb{K}
, then the
product
ab^{-1}
will be denoted by
a/b
and by
\dfrac{a}{b}
. Note that
this is always defined (unlike when
\mathbb{K}
is a field).

•

Finite products
\prod\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}
of elements of a semifield are
defined in the same way as in commutative rings. The same applies to finite
sums
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}
as long as they are nonempty (i.e., as long as

I\neq\varnothing
). The empty sum is not defined in a semifield, since there
is no zero element.

3.2
The birational involution

For the rest of Section
3
, we agree to the following two conventions:

Convention 3.2
.

We fix a positive integer
n
and a semifield
\mathbb{K}
.
We also fix an
n
-tuple
u\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
.

Convention 3.3
.

If
a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
is an
n
-tuple, and if

i\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
a_{i}
shall denote the
i^{\prime}
-th entry of
a
,
where
i^{\prime}
is the unique element of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}

satisfying
i^{\prime}\equiv i\operatorname{mod}n
. Thus, each
n
-tuple

a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
satisfies
a=\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\right)

and
a_{i}=a_{i+n}
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, if
a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
is any
n
-tuple, then the family
\left(a_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
n
-periodic.

We shall soon use the letter
x
for an
n
-tuple in
\mathbb{K}^{n}
; thus,

x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
will be the entries of this
n
-tuple. This has
nothing to do with the indeterminates
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
from Section

1
(that unfortunately use the same letters); we actually

forget all conventions from Section
1
(apart from

\mathbb{N}=\left\{0,1,2,\ldots\right\}
) for the entire Section

3
.

The following is obvious:

Lemma 3.4
.

If
a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
is any
n
-tuple, then
a_{k+1}a_{k+2}\cdots a_{k+n}=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 3.5
.

We define a map
\mathbf{f}_{u}:\mathbb{K}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}^{n}
as follows:

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and

r\in\mathbb{N}
, define an element
t_{r,j}\in\mathbb{K}
by

t_{r,j}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}\underbrace{x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}x_{j+i}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=k+1}^{r}u_{j+i}}.

Define
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
by setting

y_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Set
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
.

Example 3.6
.

Set
n=4
for this example. Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be an

n
-tuple; thus,
x=\left(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\right)
. Let us see what
the definition of
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
in Definition
3.5

boils down to in this case.

Let us first compute the elements
t_{n-1,j}=t_{3,j}
from Definition

3.5
. The definition of
t_{3,0}
yields

\displaystyle t_{3,0}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{3}x_{0+1}x_{0+2}\cdots x_{0+k}\cdot u_{0+k+1}u_{0+k+2}\cdots u_{0+3}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{3}x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{k}\cdot u_{k+1}u_{k+2}\cdots u_{3}

\displaystyle=u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle t_{3,1}

\displaystyle=u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}u_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}x_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}x_{3}x_{4};

\displaystyle t_{3,2}

\displaystyle=u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}+x_{3}u_{4}u_{5}+x_{3}x_{4}u_{5}+x_{3}x_{4}x_{5}

\displaystyle=u_{3}u_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}u_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}x_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}x_{4}x_{1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }u_{5}=u_{1}\text{ and }x_{5}=x_{1}\right);

\displaystyle t_{3,3}

\displaystyle=u_{4}u_{5}u_{6}+x_{4}u_{5}u_{6}+x_{4}x_{5}u_{6}+x_{4}x_{5}x_{6}

\displaystyle=u_{4}u_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}u_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}x_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}x_{1}x_{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }u_{5}=u_{1}\text{ and }x_{5}=x_{1}\text{ and }u_{6}=u_{2}\text{ and }x_{6}=x_{2}\right).

We don’t need to compute any further
t_{3,j}
’s, since we can easily see that

t_{3,j}=t_{3,j^{\prime}}\text{ for any integers }j\text{ and }j^{\prime}\text{
satisfying }j\equiv j^{\prime}\operatorname{mod}4.

(2)

Thus, in particular,
t_{3,4}=t_{3,0}
and
t_{3,5}=t_{3,1}
.

Now, let us compute the
4
-tuple
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}=\mathbb{K}^{4}
from
Definition
3.5
. By its definition, we have

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=u_{1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{1-1}t_{3,1-1}}{x_{1+1}t_{3,1+1}}=u_{1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{0}t_{3,0}}{x_{2}t_{3,2}}=u_{1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{4}t_{3,0}}{x_{2}t_{3,2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }u_{0}=u_{4}\right)

\displaystyle=u_{1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{4}\left(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{x_{2}\left(u_{3}u_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}u_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}x_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}x_{4}x_{1}\right)}

(by our formulas for
t_{3,0}
and
t_{3,2}
). Similar computations lead to

\displaystyle y_{2}

\displaystyle=u_{2}\cdot\dfrac{u_{1}\left(u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}u_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}x_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}\right)}{x_{3}\left(u_{4}u_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}u_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}x_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}x_{1}x_{2}\right)};

\displaystyle y_{3}

\displaystyle=u_{3}\cdot\dfrac{u_{2}\left(u_{3}u_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}u_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}x_{4}u_{1}+x_{3}x_{4}x_{1}\right)}{x_{4}\left(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\right)};

\displaystyle y_{4}

\displaystyle=u_{4}\cdot\dfrac{u_{3}\left(u_{4}u_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}u_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}x_{1}u_{2}+x_{4}x_{1}x_{2}\right)}{x_{1}\left(u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}u_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}x_{3}u_{4}+x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}\right)}.

Of course, knowing one of these four equalities is enough; the expression for

y_{i+1}
is obtained from the expression for
y_{i}
by shifting all indices
(other than the “
3
”s that were originally
“
n-1
”s) forward by
1
.

Remark 3.7
.

Instead of assuming
\mathbb{K}
to be a semifield, we could have assumed that

\mathbb{K}
is an infinite field. In that case, the
\mathbf{f}_{u}
in
Definition
3.5
would be a birational map instead of a map in the usual
sense of this word, since the denominators
x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}
in the
definition of
y
can be zero. Everything we say below about
\mathbf{f}_{u}

would nevertheless still hold on the level of birational maps.

The map
\mathbf{f}_{u}
we just defined has the following properties:

Theorem 3.8
.

(a)

The map
\mathbf{f}_{u}
is an involution (i.e., we have

\mathbf{f}_{u}\circ\mathbf{f}_{u}=\operatorname*{id}
).

(b)

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be such
that
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
. Then,

y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}\cdot x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}.

(c)

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be such
that
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
. Then,

\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}\right)=\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right)

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(d)

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be such
that
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
. Then,

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{u_{i}+x_{i}}{x_{i}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{u_{i}+y_{i}}{u_{i}}.

Theorem
3.8
will be crucial for us; but before we can prove it,
we will need a few lemmas.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. Let
t_{r,j}
and

y
be as in Definition
3.5
. Then:

(a)

We have
t_{r,j}=t_{r,j^{\prime}}
for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
and any two integers
j
and
j^{\prime}
satisfying
j\equiv j^{\prime}\operatorname{mod}n
. In other words, for each
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the
family
\left(t_{r,j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
n
-periodic.

(b)

We have
t_{0,j}=1
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(c)

For each
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

x_{j}t_{r,j}+u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}=t_{r+1,j-1}.

(d)

For each
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

u_{j+r+1}t_{r,j}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+r+1}=t_{r+1,j}.

(e)

For each
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

x_{a}t_{n-1,a}+u_{b-1}t_{n-1,b-1}=x_{b}t_{n-1,b}+u_{a-1}t_{n-1,a-1}.

(f)

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}+u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}=\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}.

(g)

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and each positive integer
q
,
we have

t_{n-1,j+q+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+q+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{q-1,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{q,j}.

(h)

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

y_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}.

Now, for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
, let us define an element

t_{r,j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}
by

t_{r,j}^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}\underbrace{y_{j+1}y_{j+2}\cdots y_{j+k}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}y_{j+i}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=k+1}^{r}u_{j+i}}.

(This is precisely how
t_{r,j}
was defined, except that we are using
y
in
place of
x
now.) Then:

(i)

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\dfrac{t_{q,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+q}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+q+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{q,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+q+1}}.

(j)

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}u_{j}}{u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}x_{j+1}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}.

(k)

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

x_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}}{y_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}}.

Proof 3.10

(Proof of Lemma
3.9
.)
.

The proof of this lemma is long but
unsophisticated: Each part follows by rather straightforward computations
(and, in the cases of parts
(g)
and
(i)
, an induction on

q
) from the previously proven parts. We shall show the details, but a
computationally inclined reader may have a better time reconstructing them
independently.
1
1

1

We note that the hardest parts of the proof – namely,
the proofs of parts
(g)
,
(i)
,
(j)
and
(k)

– can be sidestepped entirely, as these parts will only be used in the proof
of Theorem
3.8

(a)
, but we will give an alternative
proof of Theorem
3.8

(a)
later on (in Remark

3.18
), which avoids using them.

(a)
Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
. The definition of
t_{r,j}
shows that

t_{r,j}=t_{r,j+n}
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
(since each
i\in\mathbb{Z}

satisfies
u_{i}=u_{i+n}
and
x_{i}=x_{i+n}
). Thus, the family
\left(t_{r,j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
n
-periodic. This yields the claim of
Lemma
3.9

(a)
.

(b)
Trivial consequence of the definition of
t_{0,j}
.

(c)
Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, the definition
of
t_{r+1,j-1}
yields

\displaystyle t_{r+1,j-1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r+1}x_{j}x_{j+1}\cdots x_{j+k-1}\cdot u_{j+k}u_{j+k+1}\cdots u_{j+r}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{x_{j}x_{j+1}\cdots x_{j-1}}_{=\left(\text{empty
product}\right)=1}\cdot u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r+1}\underbrace{x_{j}x_{j+1}\cdots x_{j+k-1}}_{=x_{j}\cdot x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k-1}}\cdot u_{j+k}u_{j+k+1}\cdots u_{j+r}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have split off the addend for
}k=0\text{ from the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r+1}x_{j}\cdot x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k-1}\cdot u_{j+k}u_{j+k+1}\cdots u_{j+r}}_{=x_{j}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r+1}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k-1}\cdot u_{j+k}u_{j+k+1}\cdots u_{j+r}}

\displaystyle=u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}+x_{j}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r+1}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k-1}\cdot u_{j+k}u_{j+k+1}\cdots u_{j+r}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}\\
\text{(here, we have substituted }k\text{ for }k-1\text{ in the
sum)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}+x_{j}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{r,j}\\
\text{(by the definition of }t_{r,j}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}+x_{j}t_{r,j}=x_{j}t_{r,j}+u_{j}u_{j+1}\cdots u_{j+r}.

This proves Lemma
3.9

(c)
.

(d)
Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, the definition
of
t_{r+1,j}
yields

\displaystyle t_{r+1,j}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r+1}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}_{=u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}\cdot u_{j+r+1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+r+1}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+\left(r+1\right)+1}u_{j+\left(r+1\right)+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}_{=\left(\text{empty product}\right)=1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have split off the addend for
}k=r+1\text{ from the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}\cdot u_{j+r+1}}_{=u_{j+r+1}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+r+1}

\displaystyle=u_{j+r+1}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+r}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{r,j}\\
\text{(by the
definition of }t_{r,j}\text{)}\end{subarray}}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+r+1}

\displaystyle=u_{j+r+1}t_{r,j}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+r+1}.

This proves Lemma
3.9

(d)
.

(e)
We WLOG assume that
n\neq 1
, since otherwise the claim is easy
to check by hand. Thus,
n\geq 2
, so that
n-2\in\mathbb{N}
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, Lemma
3.9

(c)
(applied to
r=n-2
and
j=a
) yields

x_{a}t_{n-2,a}+u_{a}u_{a+1}\cdots u_{a+n-2}=t_{\left(n-2\right)+1,a-1}=t_{n-1,a-1}

(since
\left(n-2\right)+1=n-1
). Multiplying both sides of this equality
by
u_{a-1}
, we obtain

u_{a-1}\left(x_{a}t_{n-2,a}+u_{a}u_{a+1}\cdots u_{a+n-2}\right)=u_{a-1}t_{n-1,a-1}.

Hence,

\displaystyle u_{a-1}t_{n-1,a-1}

\displaystyle=u_{a-1}\left(x_{a}t_{n-2,a}+u_{a}u_{a+1}\cdots u_{a+n-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{u_{a-1}x_{a}}_{=x_{a}u_{a-1}}t_{n-2,a}+\underbrace{u_{a-1}\cdot u_{a}u_{a+1}\cdots u_{a+n-2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{a-1}u_{a}\cdots u_{a+n-2}\\
=u_{\left(a-2\right)+1}u_{\left(a-2\right)+2}\cdots u_{\left(a-2\right)+n}\\
=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.aprod})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=x_{a}u_{a-1}t_{n-2,a}+u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}.

(3)

Also, Lemma
3.9

(d)
(applied to
r=n-2
and
j=b
)
yields

u_{b+\left(n-2\right)+1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{b+1}x_{b+2}\cdots x_{b+\left(n-2\right)+1}=t_{\left(n-2\right)+1,b}.

In view of
\left(n-2\right)+1=n-1
, this rewrites as

u_{b+n-1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{b+1}x_{b+2}\cdots x_{b+n-1}=t_{n-1,b}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
x_{b}
, we obtain

x_{b}\left(u_{b+n-1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{b+1}x_{b+2}\cdots x_{b+n-1}\right)=x_{b}t_{n-1,b},

so that

\displaystyle x_{b}t_{n-1,b}

\displaystyle=x_{b}\left(u_{b+n-1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{b+1}x_{b+2}\cdots x_{b+n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=x_{b}\underbrace{u_{b+n-1}}_{=u_{\left(b-1\right)+n}=u_{b-1}}t_{n-2,b}+\underbrace{x_{b}\cdot x_{b+1}x_{b+2}\cdots x_{b+n-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{b}x_{b+1}\cdots x_{b+n-1}\\
=x_{\left(b-1\right)+1}x_{\left(b-1\right)+2}\cdots x_{\left(b-1\right)+n}\\
=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.aprod})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=x_{b}u_{b-1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}.

Adding (
3
) to this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle x_{b}t_{n-1,b}+u_{a-1}t_{n-1,a-1}

\displaystyle=x_{b}u_{b-1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}+x_{a}u_{a-1}t_{n-2,a}+u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}

(4)

\displaystyle=x_{a}u_{a-1}t_{n-2,a}+x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}+x_{b}u_{b-1}t_{n-2,b}+u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}.

The same argument (applied to
b
and
a
instead of
a
and
b
) yields

\displaystyle x_{a}t_{n-1,a}+u_{b-1}t_{n-1,b-1}

\displaystyle=x_{b}u_{b-1}t_{n-2,b}+x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}+x_{a}u_{a-1}t_{n-2,a}+u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}.

Comparing this with (
4
), we obtain
x_{a}t_{n-1,a}+u_{b-1}t_{n-1,b-1}=x_{b}t_{n-1,b}+u_{a-1}t_{n-1,a-1}
. This proves
Lemma
3.9

(e)
.

(f)
Applying Lemma
3.9

(e)
to
a=i+1
and

b=i
, we obtain

\displaystyle x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}+u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}

\displaystyle=x_{i}t_{n-1,i}+\underbrace{u_{i+1-1}}_{=u_{i}}\underbrace{t_{n-1,i+1-1}}_{=t_{n-1,i}}

\displaystyle=x_{i}t_{n-1,i}+u_{i}t_{n-1,i}=\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}.

This proves Lemma
3.9

(f)
.

(g)
We shall prove Lemma
3.9

(g)
by induction
on
q
:

Induction base:
Let us show that Lemma
3.9

(g)

holds for
q=1
.

Indeed, let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. The definition of
t_{1,j}
yields

t_{1,j}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{1}x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}\cdot u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+1}=x_{j+1}+u_{j+1}.

Hence,

t_{n-1,j+1}\underbrace{t_{1,j}}_{=x_{j+1}+u_{j+1}}=t_{n-1,j+1}\left(x_{j+1}+u_{j+1}\right)=\left(x_{j+1}+u_{j+1}\right)t_{n-1,j+1}.

Comparing this with

\displaystyle\underbrace{t_{n-1,j+1+1}}_{=t_{n-1,j+2}}\cdot\underbrace{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+2}\\
=x_{j+2}\end{subarray}}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}\underbrace{t_{1-1,j+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{0,j+1}=1\\
\text{(by
Lemma \ref{lem.f.steps} {(b)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=t_{n-1,j+2}x_{j+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}=x_{j+2}t_{n-1,j+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}=\left(x_{j+1}+u_{j+1}\right)t_{n-1,j+1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.f.steps} {(f)},
applied to }i=j+1\right),

we obtain

t_{n-1,j+1+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{1-1,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{1,j}.

Now, forget that we fixed
j
. We thus have proved that

t_{n-1,j+1+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{1-1,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{1,j}

for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. In other words, Lemma
3.9

(g)
holds for
q=1
. This completes the induction base.
2
2

2

We
could have simplified this part of the proof by taking
q=0
as induction base
instead. But this would have required extending the semifield
\mathbb{K}
to
a semiring
\mathbb{K}\sqcup\left\{0\right\}
by adjoining a zero (since

t_{-1,j}
would be an empty sum). It is not hard to do this, but we prefer
computations to technicalities.

Induction step:
Fix a positive integer
p
. Assume (as induction
hypothesis) that Lemma
3.9

(g)
holds for
q=p
. We must
now show that Lemma
3.9

(g)
holds for
q=p+1
.

We have assumed that Lemma
3.9

(g)
holds for
q=p
. In
other words, each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies

t_{n-1,j+p+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{p,j}.

(5)

Now, let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
be arbitrary. Then, Lemma
3.9

(d)
(applied to
r=p
) yields

u_{j+p+1}t_{p,j}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+p+1}=t_{p+1,j}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
t_{n-1,j+1}
, we obtain

t_{n-1,j+1}\left(u_{j+p+1}t_{p,j}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+p+1}\right)=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{p+1,j}.

Hence,

\displaystyle t_{n-1,j+1}t_{p+1,j}

\displaystyle=t_{n-1,j+1}\left(u_{j+p+1}t_{p,j}+x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+p+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{t_{n-1,j+1}u_{j+p+1}}_{=u_{j+p+1}t_{n-1,j+1}}t_{p,j}+t_{n-1,j+1}\cdot x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+p+1}

\displaystyle=u_{j+p+1}\underbrace{t_{n-1,j+1}t_{p,j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{n-1,j+p+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.f.steps.g.IH}))}\end{subarray}}+t_{n-1,j+1}\cdot\underbrace{x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+p+1}}_{=x_{j+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}}

\displaystyle=u_{j+p+1}\left(t_{n-1,j+p+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +t_{n-1,j+1}\cdot x_{j+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}

\displaystyle=u_{j+p+1}t_{n-1,j+p+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j+p+1}u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +t_{n-1,j+1}\cdot x_{j+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(u_{j+p+1}t_{n-1,j+p+1}+t_{n-1,j+1}\cdot x_{j+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{j+1}t_{n-1,j+1}+u_{j+p+1}t_{n-1,j+p+1}\end{subarray}}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j+p+1}u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(x_{j+1}t_{n-1,j+1}+u_{j+p+1}t_{n-1,j+p+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{j+p+2}t_{n-1,j+p+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.f.steps} {(e)}, applied to }a=j+1\text{ and }b=j+p+2\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j+p+1}u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\left(x_{j+p+2}t_{n-1,j+p+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}\right)\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j+p+1}u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}

\displaystyle=x_{j+p+2}t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +u_{j+p+1}u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p-1,j+1}

\displaystyle=u_{j}t_{n-1,j}\underbrace{\left(u_{j+p+1}t_{p-1,j+1}+x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{p,j+1}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.f.steps} {(d)}, applied to }j+1\text{ and }p-1\text{ instead
of }j\text{ and }r\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\underbrace{x_{j+p+2}t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}}_{=t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}\cdot x_{j+p+2}}

\displaystyle=u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p,j+1}+t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot\underbrace{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+1}\cdot x_{j+p+2}}_{=x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+2}}

\displaystyle=u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p,j+1}+t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+2}

\displaystyle=t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p,j+1}.

In other words,

t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{p+1,j}.

Now, forget that we fixed
j
. We thus have proved that each
j\in\mathbb{Z}

satisfies

t_{n-1,j+p+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+p+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{p,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{p+1,j}.

In other words, Lemma
3.9

(g)
holds for
q=p+1
. This
completes the induction step. Hence, Lemma
3.9

(g)
is
proved by induction.

(h)
For
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, the claim of Lemma

3.9

(h)
follows from the definition of
y
. Thus, it
also holds for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, since any integers
j
and
j^{\prime}

satisfying
j\equiv j^{\prime}\operatorname{mod}n
satisfy
x_{j}=x_{j^{\prime}}
and
u_{j}=u_{j^{\prime}}
and
t_{n-1,j}=t_{n-1,j^{\prime}}

(by Lemma
3.9

(a)
) and
y_{j}=y_{j^{\prime}}
. Thus,
Lemma
3.9

(h)
is proved.

(i)
We shall prove Lemma
3.9

(i)
by induction
on
q
:

Induction base:
For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\dfrac{t_{0,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{0,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1}}

3

3

Proof.
Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Lemma
3.9

(b)
yields
t_{0,j}=1
. Similarly,
t_{0,j}^{\prime}=1
. From this
equality, and from
u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j}=\left(\text{empty
product}\right)=1
, we obtain

\dfrac{t_{0,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j}}=\dfrac{1}{1}.

Comparing this with

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{0,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{0,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1}}=\dfrac{1}{1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }t_{0,j}=1\text{ and }x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1}=\left(\text{empty product}\right)=1\right),

we obtain
\dfrac{t_{0,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{0,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+1}}
.
Qed.

. In other words, Lemma
3.9

(i)
holds for
q=0
.
This completes the induction base.

Induction step:
Fix
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume (as induction
hypothesis) that Lemma
3.9

(i)
holds for
q=r
. We must
now show that Lemma
3.9

(i)
holds for
q=r+1
.

We have assumed that Lemma
3.9

(i)
holds for
q=r
. In
other words, each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies

\dfrac{t_{r,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+r+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+1}}.

(6)

Now, let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
be arbitrary. Then, (
6
)
(applied to
j+1
instead of
j
) yields

\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}}{u_{j+2}u_{j+3}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+2}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}.

(7)

But Lemma
3.9

(c)
(applied to
j+1
instead of
j
)
yields

x_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}+u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}=t_{r+1,j}.

Hence,

t_{r+1,j}=x_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}+u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}=u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}+x_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}.

The same reasoning (applied to
y
and
t_{r,j}^{\prime}
instead of
x
and

t_{r,j}
) yields

t_{r+1,j}^{\prime}=u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}+y_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{r+1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}+y_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}

\displaystyle=1+\underbrace{\dfrac{y_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\dfrac{y_{j+1}t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}\cdot u_{j+2}u_{j+3}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}\\
=\dfrac{y_{j+1}}{u_{j+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}}{u_{j+2}u_{j+3}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=1+\dfrac{y_{j+1}}{u_{j+1}}\cdot\underbrace{\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}^{\prime}}{u_{j+2}u_{j+3}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+2}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.f.steps.i.short.IH3}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=1+\dfrac{y_{j+1}}{u_{j+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+2}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}.

(8)

But Lemma
3.9

(h)
(applied to
i=j+1
) yields

y_{j+1}=u_{j+1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{j}t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}t_{n-1,j+2}}.

Dividing this equality by
u_{j+1}
, we find

\dfrac{y_{j+1}}{u_{j+1}}=\dfrac{u_{j}t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}t_{n-1,j+2}}.

Hence, (
8
) becomes

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{r+1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}

\displaystyle=1+\underbrace{\dfrac{y_{j+1}}{u_{j+1}}}_{=\dfrac{u_{j}t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}t_{n-1,j+2}}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+2}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}

\displaystyle=1+\dfrac{u_{j}t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}t_{n-1,j+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+2}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}

\displaystyle=1+\dfrac{u_{j}t_{n-1,j}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+2}\cdot x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}

\displaystyle=1+\dfrac{u_{j}t_{n-1,j}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r,j+1}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }x_{j+2}\cdot x_{j+3}x_{j+4}\cdots x_{j+r+2}=x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+r+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{r,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}.

(9)

But Lemma
3.9

(g)
(applied to
q=r+1
) yields

t_{n-1,j+r+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{r,j+1}=t_{n-1,j+1}t_{r+1,j}.

(10)

Hence, (
9
) becomes

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{r+1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+r+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}+u_{j}t_{n-1,j}t_{r,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}t_{r+1,j}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.f.steps.i.short.8})}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r+1,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}.

Forget that we fixed
j
. We thus have shown that each
j\in\mathbb{Z}

satisfies

\dfrac{t_{r+1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+r+1}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+r+2}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{r+1,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+r+2}}.

In other words, Lemma
3.9

(i)
holds for
q=r+1
. This
completes the induction step. Hence, Lemma
3.9

(i)
is
proved by induction.

(j)
Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,
u_{j+n}=u_{j}
(by Convention

3.3
). Lemma
3.9

(i)
(applied to

q=n-1
) yields

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+\left(n-1\right)+1}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+\left(n-1\right)+1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+n}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}}

(11)

(since
\left(n-1\right)+1=n
). But
j\equiv j+n\operatorname{mod}n
;
hence, Lemma
3.9

(a)
(applied to
r=n-1
and

j^{\prime}=j+n
) yields
t_{n-1,j}=t_{n-1,j+n}
. Hence,
(
11
) becomes

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+n}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{t_{n-1,j+n}}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+n}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }t_{n-1,j}=t_{n-1,j+n}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}x_{j+1}}{x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}\cdot x_{j+1}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}x_{j+1}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}

(since
x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}\cdot x_{j+1}=x_{j+1}\cdot x_{j+2}x_{j+3}\cdots x_{j+n}=x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+n}=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}
(by
Lemma
3.4
)). Hence,

\displaystyle\dfrac{t_{n-1,j+1}x_{j+1}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}u_{j+n}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}\cdot u_{j+n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}u_{j+n}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}\cdot u_{j+n}=u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}u_{j}}{u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }u_{j+n}=u_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}u_{j}}{u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}

(since
u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{j+n}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}
(by Lemma

3.4
)). This proves Lemma
3.9

(j)
.

(k)
Let
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Applying Lemma
3.9

(j)
to
j=i-1
, we obtain

\dfrac{t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}u_{i-1}}{u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,i}x_{i}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}.

Applying Lemma
3.9

(j)
to
j=i+1
, we obtain

\dfrac{t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}u_{i+1}}{u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,i+2}x_{i+2}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}.

Dividing the former equality by the latter, we obtain

\dfrac{t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}u_{i-1}}{u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}/\dfrac{t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}u_{i+1}}{u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,i}x_{i}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}/\dfrac{t_{n-1,i+2}x_{i+2}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}.

This rewrites as

\dfrac{t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}u_{i-1}}{t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}u_{i+1}}=\dfrac{t_{n-1,i}x_{i}}{t_{n-1,i+2}x_{i+2}}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
u_{i+1}
, we obtain

\dfrac{t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}u_{i-1}}{t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}}=u_{i+1}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,i}x_{i}}{t_{n-1,i+2}x_{i+2}}.

(12)

Now,

\displaystyle u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}}{y_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\dfrac{t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}u_{i-1}}{t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i+1}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,i}x_{i}}{t_{n-1,i+2}x_{i+2}}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.f.steps.k.short.4}))}\end{subarray}}\cdot u_{i}/\underbrace{y_{i+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i+1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i}t_{n-1,i}}{x_{i+2}t_{n-1,i+2}}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.f.steps} {(h)},}\\
\text{applied to }i+1\text{ instead of }i\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=u_{i+1}\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,i}x_{i}}{t_{n-1,i+2}x_{i+2}}\cdot u_{i}/\left(u_{i+1}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i}t_{n-1,i}}{x_{i+2}t_{n-1,i+2}}\right)=x_{i}.

This proves Lemma
3.9

(k)
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. For each

j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

q_{j}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{x_{j+1}x_{j+2}\cdots x_{j+k}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}x_{j+i}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=k+1}^{n-1}u_{j+i}}.

Let
z\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be such that

z_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}q_{i-1}}{x_{i+1}q_{i+1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Then,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=z
.

Proof 3.12

(Proof of Lemma
3.11
.)
.

Let
t_{r,j}
and
y
be as in Definition

3.5
. Then,
t_{n-1,j}=q_{j}
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
(by comparing
the definitions of
t_{n-1,j}
and
q_{j}
). Hence,
z_{i}=y_{i}
for each

i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
(by comparing the definitions of
z_{i}

and
y_{i}
). Hence,
z=y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
(since

\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
was defined to be
y
). This proves Lemma

3.11
.

For future convenience, let us restate Lemma
3.11
with different labels:

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. For each

j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

r_{j}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{y_{j+1}y_{j+2}\cdots y_{j+k}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}y_{j+i}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=k+1}^{n-1}u_{j+i}}.

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be such that

x_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}r_{i-1}}{y_{i+1}r_{i+1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Then,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(y\right)=x
.

Proof 3.14

(Proof of Lemma
3.13
.)
.

Lemma
3.13
is just Lemma

3.11
, with
x
,
q_{j}
and
z
renamed as
y
,
r_{j}
and
x
.

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
3.8
:

Proof 3.15

(Proof of Theorem
3.8
.)
.

(a)
Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. We
shall prove that
\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\circ\mathbf{f}_{u}\right)\left(x\right)=x
.

Let
t_{r,j}
and
y
be as in Definition
3.5
. Then,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
(by the definition of
\mathbf{f}_{u}
), so that

y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
. Let
t_{r,j}^{\prime}
(for each

r\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
) be as in Lemma
3.9
. The
definition of
t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}
shows that

t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{y_{j+1}y_{j+2}\cdots y_{j+k}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}y_{j+i}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{j+k+1}u_{j+k+2}\cdots u_{j+n-1}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=k+1}^{n-1}u_{j+i}}

for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Lemma
3.9

(k)
shows that

x_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}^{\prime}}{y_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}^{\prime}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Thus, Lemma
3.13
(applied to
r_{j}=t_{n-1,j}^{\prime}
) yields
that
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(y\right)=x
. Hence,
x=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(\underbrace{y}_{=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)}\right)=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)\right)=\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\circ\mathbf{f}_{u}\right)\left(x\right)
. In other words,
\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\circ\mathbf{f}_{u}\right)\left(x\right)=x
.

Forget that we fixed
x
. We thus have proved that
\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\circ\mathbf{f}_{u}\right)\left(x\right)=x
for each
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. In other words,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\circ\mathbf{f}_{u}=\operatorname*{id}
. In other words,
\mathbf{f}_{u}
is an involution. This
proves Theorem
3.8

(a)
.

(b)
Let
t_{r,j}
be as in Definition
3.5
. Note that the
y

from Definition
3.5
is precisely the
y
in Theorem
3.8

(b)
(because both
y
’s satisfy
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
).

Lemma
3.9

(a)
yields
t_{n-1,0}=t_{n-1,n}
(since

0\equiv n\operatorname{mod}n
) and
t_{n-1,1}=t_{n-1,n+1}
(since
1\equiv n+1\operatorname{mod}n
). Multiplying these two equalities, we obtain

t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}=t_{n-1,n}t_{n-1,n+1}
, whence

\dfrac{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}}{t_{n-1,n}t_{n-1,n+1}}=1.

(13)

We have

\displaystyle y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{y_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\\
\text{(by the definition of }y\\
\text{in Definition \ref{def.f})}\end{subarray}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i-1}\right)\cdot\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i-1}\right)}{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i+1}\right)\cdot\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i+1}\right)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\right)}_{=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{n-1}\\
=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\\
\text{(by
Lemma \ref{lem.aprod})}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}\cdots t_{n-1,n-1}\\
=\dfrac{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}\cdots t_{n-1,n+1}}{t_{n-1,n}t_{n-1,n+1}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ /\left(\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{2}x_{3}\cdots x_{n+1}\\
=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.aprod})}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{n-1,2}t_{n-1,3}\cdots t_{n-1,n+1}\\
=\dfrac{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}\cdots t_{n-1,n+1}}{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)\cdot\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}\cdots t_{n-1,n+1}}{t_{n-1,n}t_{n-1,n+1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ /\left(\left(x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\right)\cdot\dfrac{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}\cdots t_{n-1,n+1}}{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}\cdot\underbrace{\dfrac{t_{n-1,0}t_{n-1,1}}{t_{n-1,n}t_{n-1,n+1}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.f.full.b.1}))}\end{subarray}}=\dfrac{\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}},

so that

y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}\cdot x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}.

This proves Theorem
3.8

(b)
.

(c)
Let
t_{r,j}
be as in Definition
3.5
. Note that the
y

from Definition
3.5
is precisely the
y
in Theorem
3.8

(c)
(because both
y
’s satisfy
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
).

Let
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

\displaystyle u_{i}+\underbrace{y_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.f.steps} {(h)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=u_{i}+u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}=u_{i}\left(1+\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\right)=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}+u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\underbrace{\left(x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}+u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.f.steps} {(f)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}.

(14)

Now,

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{u_{i}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.f.full.c.1}))}\end{subarray}}/\left(u_{i}\cdot y_{i}\right)=\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}/\left(u_{i}\cdot y_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}/\underbrace{y_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.f.steps} {(h)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}/\left(u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\right)=\dfrac{\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}}{u_{i}u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}.

The same argument (applied to
i+1
instead of
i
) yields

\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}=\dfrac{\left(x_{i+1}+u_{i+1}\right)t_{n-1,i+1}}{u_{i+1}u_{\left(i+1\right)-1}t_{n-1,\left(i+1\right)-1}}=\dfrac{\left(x_{i+1}+u_{i+1}\right)t_{n-1,i+1}}{u_{i+1}u_{i}t_{n-1,i}}

(since
\left(i+1\right)-1=i
). Multiplying (
14
) with
this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}\cdot\dfrac{\left(x_{i+1}+u_{i+1}\right)t_{n-1,i+1}}{u_{i+1}u_{i}t_{n-1,i}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)}_{=u_{i}+x_{i}}\cdot\underbrace{\dfrac{x_{i+1}+u_{i+1}}{x_{i+1}u_{i+1}}}_{=\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}}=\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}\right).

This proves Theorem
3.8

(c)
.

(d)
Let
t_{r,j}
be as in Definition
3.5
. Note that the
y

from Definition
3.5
is precisely the
y
in Theorem
3.8

(d)
(because both
y
’s satisfy
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
).

We have

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i+1}

\displaystyle=x_{2}x_{3}\cdots x_{n+1}=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.aprod}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}

(15)

and

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i+1}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=2}^{n+1}t_{n-1,i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have substituted }i\text{ for
}i+1\text{ in the product}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{t_{n-1,n+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t_{n-1,1}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.f.steps} {(a)},}\\
\text{since }n+1\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}n\text{)}\end{subarray}}\prod\limits_{i=2}^{n}t_{n-1,i}=t_{n-1,1}\prod\limits_{i=2}^{n}t_{n-1,i}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i}.

(16)

Every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies (
14
) (as we have shown
in the proof of Theorem
3.8

(c)
above). Hence,

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.f.full.c.1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{u_{i}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)t_{n-1,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}}{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i+1}\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i+1}\right)}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}}{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i}\right)}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}t_{n-1,i}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.thm.f.full.d.1}) and (\ref{pf.thm.f.full.d.2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\right)\underbrace{\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}+u_{i}\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}}_{=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{x_{i}+u_{i}}{x_{i}}}=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\right)\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{x_{i}+u_{i}}{x_{i}}.

Thus,

\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\dfrac{x_{i}+u_{i}}{x_{i}}}_{=\dfrac{u_{i}+x_{i}}{x_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{u_{i}+x_{i}}{x_{i}},

so that

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{u_{i}+x_{i}}{x_{i}}=\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{u_{i}+y_{i}}{u_{i}}.

This proves Theorem
3.8

(d)
.

Let us observe one more property of the involution
\mathbf{f}_{u}
(even
though we will have no use for it):

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be such that
x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}
. Then,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=x
.

Proof 3.17

(Proof of Proposition
3.16
.)
.

Let
t_{r,j}
and
y
be as in
Definition
3.5
. Then,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
(by the
definition of
\mathbf{f}_{u}
).

Let
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. We shall first show that
u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}=x_{i}t_{n-1,i}
.

Indeed, the definition of
t_{n-1,i}
yields

t_{n-1,i}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i+1}x_{i+2}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
x_{i}
, we find

\displaystyle x_{i}t_{n-1,i}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i+1}x_{i+2}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{x_{i}\cdot x_{i+1}x_{i+2}\cdots x_{i+k}}_{=x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k}}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+n-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.aprod})}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{i+n}u_{i+n+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}}_{=\left(\text{empty product}\right)=1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-2}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have split
off the addend for }k=n-1\text{ from the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}}_{=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-2}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}\\
\text{(here, we have
substituted }k-1\text{ for }k\text{ in the sum)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}.

(17)

On the other hand, we have
u_{i-1}=u_{i+n-1}
(since
i-1\equiv i+n-1\operatorname{mod}n
) and

\displaystyle t_{n-1,i-1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{x_{\left(i-1\right)+1}x_{\left(i-1\right)+2}\cdots x_{\left(i-1\right)+k}}_{=x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}}\cdot\underbrace{u_{\left(i-1\right)+k+1}u_{\left(i-1\right)+k+2}\cdots u_{\left(i-1\right)+n-1}}_{=u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }t_{n-1,i-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-2}.

Multiplying these two equalities, we obtain

\displaystyle u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}

\displaystyle=u_{i+n-1}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot\underbrace{u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-2}\cdot u_{i+n-1}}_{=u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+0-1}}_{=\left(\text{empty
product}\right)=1}\cdot\underbrace{u_{i+0}u_{i+0+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i}u_{i+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}\\
=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\\
\text{(by
Lemma \ref{lem.aprod})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have split
off the addend for }k=0\text{ from the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n-1}x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+k-1}\cdot u_{i+k}u_{i+k+1}\cdots u_{i+n-1}.

Comparing this with (
17
), we obtain

u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}=x_{i}t_{n-1,i}.

(18)

The same argument (applied to
i+1
instead of
i
) yields

u_{i}t_{n-1,i}=x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}.

(19)

Now, the definition of
y
yields

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}=u_{i}\underbrace{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{i}t_{n-1,i}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.f.equal-case.short.4}))}\end{subarray}}/\underbrace{\left(x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i}t_{n-1,i}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.f.equal-case.short.5}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=u_{i}x_{i}t_{n-1,i}/\left(u_{i}t_{n-1,i}\right)=x_{i}.

Now, forget that we fixed
i
. We thus have proved that
y_{i}=x_{i}
for each

i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, in particular,
y_{i}=x_{i}
for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. In other words,
y=x
. Hence,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y=x
. This proves Proposition
3.16
.

Remark 3.18
.

There is an alternative proof of Theorem

3.8

(a)
that avoids the use of the more complicated
parts of Lemma
3.9
(specifically, of parts
(g)
,

(i)
,
(j)
and
(k)
). Let us outline this proof:

The claim of Theorem
3.8

(a)
can be restated as the
equality
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)\right)=x

for each
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and each
u\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
(we are not
regarding
u
as fixed here). This equality boils down to a set of identities
between rational functions in the variables
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
(since each entry of
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
is a rational function in these variables, and each entry of

\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)\right)
is a
rational function in the former entries as well as
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n}
). These rational functions are subtraction-free (i.e., no subtraction signs
appear in them), and thus are defined over any semifield. But there is a
general principle saying that if we need to prove an identity between two
subtraction-free rational functions, it is sufficient to prove that it holds
over the semifield
\mathbb{Q}_{+}
from Example
3.2
.
(Indeed, this principle follows from the fact that any subtraction-free
rational function can be rewritten as a ratio of two polynomials with
nonnegative integer coefficients, and thus an identity between two
subtraction-free rational functions can be rewritten as an identity between
two such polynomials; but the latter kind of identity will necessarily be true
if it has been checked on all positive rational numbers.)

As a consequence of this discussion, in order to prove Theorem

3.8

(a)
in full generality, it suffices to prove Theorem

3.8

(a)
in the case when
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}_{+}
. So
let us restrict ourselves to this case. Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. We must show
that
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)\right)=x
.

Let
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
, and let
z=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(y\right)
. We will show that
z=x
.

Assume the contrary. Thus,
z\neq x
. Hence, there exists some
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
such that
z_{i}\neq x_{i}
. Consider this
i
. Hence,
either
z_{i}>x_{i}
or
z_{i}<x_{i}
. We WLOG assume that
z_{i}>x_{i}

(since the proof in the case of
z_{i}<x_{i}
is identical, except that all
inequality signs are reversed). But Theorem
3.8

(c)

yields

\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}\right)=\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right).

Likewise, Theorem
3.8

(c)
(applied to
y
and
z

instead of
x
and
y
) yields

\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right)=\left(u_{i}+z_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{z_{i+1}}\right)

(since
z=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(y\right)
). Hence,

\displaystyle\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(u_{i}+\underbrace{z_{i}}_{>x_{i}}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{z_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle>\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{z_{i+1}}\right).

Cancelling the positive number
u_{i}+x_{i}
from this inequality, we obtain

\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}>\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{z_{i+1}}
.
Hence,
\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}>\dfrac{1}{z_{i+1}}
, so that
z_{i+1}>x_{i+1}
.
Thus, from
z_{i}>x_{i}
, we have obtained
z_{i+1}>x_{i+1}
. The same
reasoning (but applied to
i+1
instead of
i
) now yields
z_{i+2}>x_{i+2}

(since
z_{i+1}>x_{i+1}
). Proceeding in the same way, we successively obtain

z_{i+3}>x_{i+3}
and
z_{i+4}>x_{i+4}
and
z_{i+5}>x_{i+5}
and so on.
Hence,

\underbrace{z_{i}}_{>x_{i}}\underbrace{z_{i+1}}_{>x_{i+1}}\cdots\underbrace{z_{i+n-1}}_{>x_{i+n-1}}>x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+n-1}.

(20)

But Theorem
3.8

(b)
yields

y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}\cdot x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}.

Also, Theorem
3.8

(b)
(applied to
y
and
z
instead of

x
and
y
) yields

z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n}\cdot y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}

(since
z=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(y\right)
). Comparing these two equalities,
we find
y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}\cdot x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n}\cdot y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}
, so that

x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n}.

(21)

But Lemma
3.4
yields
z_{i}z_{i+1}\cdots z_{i+n-1}=z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n}
and
x_{i}x_{i+1}\cdots x_{i+n-1}=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}
.
In light of these two equalities, we can rewrite
(
20
) as
z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n}>x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}
. This, however, contradicts (
21
). This
contradiction shows that our assumption was false, thus concluding our proof
of
z=x
.

Now,
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(\underbrace{\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)}_{=y}\right)=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(y\right)=z=x
, as we wanted to prove.
Hence, Theorem
3.8

(a)
is proved again.

We shall take up the study of the birational involution
\mathbf{f}_{u}
again
in Subsection
5.2
, where we will pose several questions about
its meaning and uniqueness properties.

4
Proof of the main theorem

We shall now slowly approach the proof of Theorem
2.2
through a
long sequence of auxiliary results, some of them easy, some well-known.

4.1
From the life of snakes

Recall the conventions introduced in Section
1
and in Convention

2.1
. Let us next introduce some further notations.

Definition 4.1
.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{L}
denote the ring
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}\right]
of Laurent polynomials
in the
n
indeterminates
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
over
\mathbf{k}
.
Clearly, the polynomial ring
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]
is a subring of
\mathcal{L}
.

(b)

We let
x_{\Pi}
denote the monomial
x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\in\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{L}
.

If
f\in\Lambda
is a symmetric function
4
4

4

or, more generally, any
formal power series in
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
that is of bounded degree
, and if
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
are
n
elements of a commutative
\mathbf{k}
-algebra

A
, then
f\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n},0,0,0,\ldots\right)
means the
result of substituting
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n},0,0,0,\ldots
for

x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},x_{n+1},x_{n+2},x_{n+3},\ldots
in
f
. This is a
well-defined element of
A
(see
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.1.2]
for the proof),
and is denoted by
f\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\right)
. It is called
the
evaluation
of
f
at
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
.

For any symmetric function
f\in\Lambda
, the evaluation

f\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=f\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},0,0,0,\ldots\right)

is a polynomial in
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]
and
thus a Laurent polynomial in
\mathcal{L}
. Moreover, for any symmetric
function
f\in\Lambda
, the evaluation

f\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)=f\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1},0,0,0,\ldots\right)

is a Laurent polynomial in
\mathcal{L}
as well.

Convention 4.1
.

For the rest of Section
4
, let us agree to the
following notation: If
\gamma
is an
n
-tuple (of any objects), then we let

\gamma_{i}
denote the
i
-th entry of
\gamma
whenever
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. Thus, each
n
-tuple
\gamma
satisfies

\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\right)
.

Definition 4.2
.

(a)

A
snake
means an
n
-tuple
