\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)
of integers (not
necessarily nonnegative) such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
.

(b)

A snake
\lambda
is said to be
nonnegative
if it
belongs to
\mathbb{N}^{n}
(that is, if all its entries are nonnegative).
Thus, a nonnegative snake is the same as a partition having length
\leq n
.
In other words, a nonnegative snake is the same as a partition
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.

(c)

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is an
n
-tuple, and
d
is
an integer, then
\lambda+d
denotes the
n
-tuple
\left(\lambda_{1}+d,\lambda_{2}+d,\ldots,\lambda_{n}+d\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(which is
obtained from
\lambda
by adding
d
to each entry), whereas
\lambda-d

denotes the
n
-tuple
\left(\lambda_{1}-d,\lambda_{2}-d,\ldots,\lambda_{n}-d\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. (Thus,
\lambda-d=\lambda+\left(-d\right)
.)

(d)

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, then
\lambda^{\vee}

denotes the
n
-tuple
\left(-\lambda_{n},-\lambda_{n-1},\ldots,-\lambda_{1}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

(e)

We regard
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module in the
obvious way. Thus, if
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

are two
n
-tuples of integers, then

\displaystyle\lambda+\mu

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1},\lambda_{2}+\mu_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}+\mu_{n}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda-\mu

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1},\lambda_{2}-\mu_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}-\mu_{n}\right).

(f)

We let
\rho
denote the nonnegative snake
\left(n-1,n-2,\ldots,2,1,0\right)
. Thus,

\rho_{i}=n-i\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

(22)

Example 4.3
.

In this example, let
n=3
.

(a)

The four
3
-tuples
\left(3,1,0\right)
,
\left(2,2,1\right)
,
\left(1,0,-1\right)
and
\left(-1,-2,-5\right)
are
examples of snakes.

(b)

The first two of these four snakes (but not the last two)
are nonnegative.

(c)

We have
\left(5,3,1\right)+3=\left(8,6,4\right)

and
\left(5,3,1\right)-3=\left(2,0,-2\right)
.

(d)

We have
\left(5,2,2\right)^{\vee}=\left(-2,-2,-5\right)
.

(e)

We have
\left(2,1,2\right)+\left(3,4,5\right)=\left(5,5,7\right)
.

(f)

We have
\rho=\left(2,1,0\right)
.

Note that what we call a “snake” here is
called a “staircase of height
n
” in
Stembridge’s work
[
Stembr87
]
, where he uses these snakes to index
finite-dimensional polynomial representations of the group
\operatorname*{GL}\nolimits_{n}\left(\mathbb{C}\right)
. We avoid calling them
“staircases”, as that word has since been
used for other things (in particular,
\rho
is often called “the
n
-staircase” in the jargon of combinatorialists).

The notations introduced in Definition
4.2
have the following properties:

Proposition 4.4
.

(a)

If
\lambda
is a snake, and
d
is an integer, then

\lambda+d
and
\lambda-d
are snakes as well.

(b)

If
\lambda
is a snake, then
\lambda^{\vee}
is a snake
as well.

(c)

We have
\left(\lambda+\mu\right)+d=\left(\lambda+d\right)+\mu
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(d)

We have
\lambda+\left(d+e\right)=\left(\lambda+d\right)+e
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
d\in\mathbb{Z}
and

e\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(e)

We have
\left(\lambda+d\right)-d=\left(\lambda-d\right)+d=\lambda
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and

d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof 4.5

(Proof of Proposition
4.4
.)
.

Completely straightforward.

Let us now assign a Laurent polynomial
a_{\lambda}
to each
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
:

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be any
n
-tuple. Then, we
define the Laurent polynomial

a_{\lambda}:=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}\in\mathcal{L},

where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is the symmetric group of the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
(and where
\left(-1\right)^{w}
denotes the sign
of a permutation
w
). This Laurent polynomial
a_{\lambda}
is called the

alternant
corresponding to the
n
-tuple
\lambda
.

(The “
a
” in the notation “
a_{\lambda}
” has nothing to do with the
a
in Theorem

2.2
.)

Example 4.7
.

We have

\displaystyle a_{\left(5,3,2\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}}\left(-1\right)^{w}x_{w\left(1\right)}^{5}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{3}x_{w\left(3\right)}^{2}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{3}x_{3}^{2}+x_{2}^{5}x_{3}^{3}x_{1}^{2}+x_{3}^{5}x_{1}^{3}x_{2}^{2}-x_{1}^{5}x_{3}^{3}x_{2}^{2}-x_{2}^{5}x_{1}^{3}x_{3}^{2}-x_{3}^{5}x_{2}^{3}x_{1}^{2}.

The sum in Definition
4.6
is the same kind of sum that appears in
the definition of a determinant. Therefore, we can rewrite the alternant as follows:

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. Then, the
alternant
a_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{L}
satisfies

a_{\lambda}=\det\left(\left(x_{j}^{\lambda_{i}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n}\right)=\det\left(\left(x_{i}^{\lambda_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n}\right).

Thus, in particular, the alternant
a_{\rho}
corresponding to the snake

\rho=\left(n-1,n-2,\ldots,2,1,0\right)=\left(n-1,n-2,\ldots,n-n\right)

satisfies

a_{\rho}=\det\left(\left(x_{i}^{n-j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n}\right)=\prod\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left(x_{i}-x_{j}\right)

(by the classical formula for the Vandermonde determinant).

We recall a standard concept from commutative algebra: An element
a
of a
commutative ring
A
is said to be
regular
if it has the property
that every
x\in A
satisfying
ax=0
must satisfy
x=0
. (Thus, regular
elements are the same as elements that are not zero-divisors, if one does not
require zero-divisors to be nonzero
5
5

5

Unfortunately, there is no
agreement in the literature on whether zero-divisors should be required to be
nonzero. This is one of the reasons why we are avoiding this notion.
.)

Lemma 4.9
.

The alternant
a_{\rho}
is a regular element of

\mathcal{L}
.

Proof 4.10

(Proof of Lemma
4.9
.)
.

Let
b\in\mathcal{L}
be such that

a_{\rho}b=0
. We want to show that
b=0
.

We know that
b
is a Laurent polynomial, and thus has the form
b=\dfrac{c}{x_{1}^{u_{1}}x_{2}^{u_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{u_{n}}}
for some
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
and some polynomial
c\in\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]
. Consider these
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n}

and this
c
. From
b=\dfrac{c}{x_{1}^{u_{1}}x_{2}^{u_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{u_{n}}}
, we obtain
c=b\cdot x_{1}^{u_{1}}x_{2}^{u_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{u_{n}}
.
Multiplying this equality by
a_{\rho}
, we obtain

a_{\rho}c=\underbrace{a_{\rho}b}_{=0}\cdot x_{1}^{u_{1}}x_{2}^{u_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{u_{n}}=0.

But it is a well-known fact (see, e.g.,
[
Grinbe19
, Corollary 4.4]
) that
the polynomial
\prod\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left(x_{i}-x_{j}\right)
is a
regular element of
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]
. In
other words,
a_{\rho}
is a regular element of
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]
(since
a_{\rho}=\prod\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left(x_{i}-x_{j}\right)
). Hence, from
a_{\rho}c=0
, we obtain
c=0
. Now,

b=\dfrac{c}{x_{1}^{u_{1}}x_{2}^{u_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{u_{n}}}=0
(since
c=0
).

Forget that we fixed
b
. We thus have shown that each
b\in\mathcal{L}

satisfying
a_{\rho}b=0
satisfies
b=0
. In other words,
a_{\rho}
is a
regular element of
\mathcal{L}
. This proves Lemma
4.9
.

Lemma
4.9
shows that fractions of the form
\dfrac{u}{a_{\rho}}
(where
u\in\mathcal{L}
) are well-defined if
u
is a multiple
of
a_{\rho}
. (That is, there is never more than one
b\in\mathcal{L}
that
satisfies
a_{\rho}b=u
.)

We notice that the element
x_{\Pi}=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}
of
\mathcal{L}

is invertible (since
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
are invertible in

\mathcal{L}
).

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be any
n
-tuple, and let

d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,
a_{\lambda+d}=x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda}
.

Proof 4.12

(Proof of Lemma
4.11
.)
.

The definition of
a_{\lambda}
yields

a_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}.

(23)

But the definition of
\lambda+d
yields
\lambda+d=\left(\lambda_{1}+d,\lambda_{2}+d,\ldots,\lambda_{n}+d\right)
. Hence, the definition of

a_{\lambda+d}
yields

\displaystyle a_{\lambda+d}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\underbrace{x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}+d}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}+d}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}+d}}_{=\left(x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}\right)\left(x_{w\left(1\right)}^{d}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{d}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{d}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\left(x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}\right)\underbrace{\left(x_{w\left(1\right)}^{d}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{d}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{d}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{1}^{d}x_{2}^{d}\cdots x_{n}^{d}\\
\text{(since
}w:\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}\rightarrow\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}\\
\text{is a bijection)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\left(x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}\right)\underbrace{\left(x_{1}^{d}x_{2}^{d}\cdots x_{n}^{d}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}\right)^{d}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\\
\text{(since }x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=x_{\Pi}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\left(x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}\right)x_{\Pi}^{d}

\displaystyle=x_{\Pi}^{d}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\lambda_{n}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=a_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.alt.move.short.alam=}))}\end{subarray}}=x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda}.

This proves Lemma
4.11
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\lambda
be a snake. Then,
a_{\lambda+\rho}
is a
multiple of
a_{\rho}
in
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof 4.14

(Proof of Lemma
4.13
.)
.

Our proof will consist of two steps:

Step 1:
We will prove Lemma
4.13
in the particular case
when
\lambda
is nonnegative.

Step 2:
We will use Lemma
4.11
to derive the general
case of Lemma
4.13
from this particular case.

We will use this strategy again further on; we shall refer to it as the

right-shift strategy
.

Here are the details of the two steps:

Step 1:
Let us prove that Lemma
4.13
holds in the
particular case when
\lambda
is nonnegative.

Indeed, let us assume that
\lambda
is nonnegative. We must show that

a_{\lambda+\rho}
is a multiple of
a_{\rho}
in
\mathcal{L}
.

We know that
\lambda
is a nonnegative snake, thus a partition of length

\leq n
. Hence,
[
GriRei20
, Corollary 2.6.7]
shows that
s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}
. Thus,

a_{\lambda+\rho}=a_{\rho}\cdot s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
. This shows that
a_{\lambda+\rho}
is a multiple of

a_{\rho}
in
\mathcal{L}
(since
s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\in\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{L}
). Thus, Lemma
4.13
is proved under the
assumption that
\lambda
is nonnegative. This completes Step 1.

Step 2:
Let us now prove Lemma
4.13
in the general case.

We know that
\lambda
is a snake. Thus,
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
. Hence, each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}

satisfies
\lambda_{i}\geq\lambda_{n}
and thus

\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{n}\geq 0.

(24)

The snake
\lambda
may or may not be nonnegative. However, there exists some
integer
d
such that the snake
\lambda+d
is nonnegative
6
6

6

Indeed,
for example, we can take
d=-\lambda_{n}
. Then, all entries of
\lambda+d

have the form
\lambda_{i}+\underbrace{d}_{=-\lambda_{n}}=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{n}
for some
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, and thus are
nonnegative (because of (
24
)); this shows that the snake

\lambda+d
is nonnegative.
. Consider this
d
. Proposition

4.4

(c)
(applied to
\mu=\rho
) yields
\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d=\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho
.

The snake
\lambda+d
is nonnegative; thus, we can apply Lemma
4.13

to
\lambda+d
instead of
\lambda
(because in Step 1, we have proved that
Lemma
4.13
holds in the particular case when
\lambda
is
nonnegative). Thus we conclude that
a_{\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho}
is a
multiple of
a_{\rho}
in
\mathcal{L}
. In other words, there exists some

u\in\mathcal{L}
such that
a_{\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho}=a_{\rho}u
.
Consider this
u
. From
\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d=\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho
, we obtain
a_{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d}=a_{\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho}=a_{\rho}u
.

Lemma
4.11
(applied to
\lambda+\rho
instead of
\lambda
)
yields
a_{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d}=x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda+\rho}
.
Since the element
x_{\Pi}
of
\mathcal{L}
is invertible, we thus obtain

a_{\lambda+\rho}=\underbrace{\left(x_{\Pi}^{d}\right)^{-1}}_{=x_{\Pi}^{-d}}\underbrace{a_{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d}}_{=a_{\rho}u}=x_{\Pi}^{-d}a_{\rho}u=a_{\rho}\cdot x_{\Pi}^{-d}u.

Hence,
a_{\lambda+\rho}
is a multiple of
a_{\rho}
(since
x_{\Pi}^{-d}u\in\mathcal{L}
). This proves Lemma
4.13
. Thus, Step 2 is
complete, and Lemma
4.13
is proven.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
\lambda
be a snake. We define an element

\overline{s}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{L}
by
\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}
. (This is well-defined, because Lemma

4.13
shows that
a_{\lambda+\rho}
is a multiple of
a_{\rho}
in

\mathcal{L}
, and because Lemma
4.9
shows that the fraction

\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}
is uniquely defined.)

It makes sense to refer to the elements
\overline{s}_{\lambda}
just defined
as “
Schur Laurent polynomials
”. In
fact, as the following lemma shows, they are identical with the Schur
polynomials
s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
when the
snake
\lambda
is nonnegative:

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.
Then,

\overline{s}_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right).

Proof 4.17

(Proof of Lemma
4.16
.)
.

We know that
\lambda
is a partition of
length
\leq n
(since
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
).
Hence,
\lambda
is a nonnegative snake. Furthermore, since
\lambda
is a
partition of length
\leq n
, we can apply
[
GriRei20
, Corollary 2.6.7]
and
obtain
s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}
(since
\overline{s}_{\lambda}
was defined to be
\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}
). This
proves Lemma
4.16
.

The Schur Laurent polynomials
\overline{s}_{\lambda}
appear in Stembridge’s

[
Stembr87
]
, where they are named
s_{\lambda}
. (The equivalence of our
definition with his follows from
[
Stembr87
, Theorem 7.1]
.)

Furthermore, from Lemma
4.11
, we can easily obtain an analogous
property for Schur Laurent polynomials:

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be any snake, and let

d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,
\overline{s}_{\lambda+d}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\lambda}
.

Proof 4.19

(Proof of Lemma
4.18
.)
.

Proposition
4.4

(c)
(applied to
\mu=\rho
) yields
\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d=\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho
. But Lemma
4.11
(applied to

\lambda+\rho
instead of
\lambda
) yields
a_{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d}=x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda+\rho}
. This rewrites as
a_{\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho}=x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda+\rho}
(since
\left(\lambda+\rho\right)+d=\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho
).

The definition of
\overline{s}_{\lambda+d}
yields

\overline{s}_{\lambda+d}=\dfrac{a_{\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=\dfrac{x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }a_{\left(\lambda+d\right)+\rho}=x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda+\rho}\right).

Comparing this with

x_{\Pi}^{d}\underbrace{\overline{s}_{\lambda}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\overline{s}_{\lambda}\text{)}\end{subarray}}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\cdot\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=\dfrac{x_{\Pi}^{d}a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}},

we obtain
\overline{s}_{\lambda+d}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\lambda}
. This
proves Lemma
4.18
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.
Then,

\overline{s}_{\mu}\overline{s}_{\nu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}.

Proof 4.21

(Proof of Lemma
4.20
.)
.

It is well-known (see, e.g.,
[
GriRei20
, Exercise
2.3.8(b)]
) that if
\lambda
is a partition having length
>n
, then

s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=0.

(25)

We know that
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
. Hence, Lemma

4.16
(applied to
\lambda=\mu
) yields
\overline{s}_{\mu}=s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
. Likewise,
\overline{s}_{\nu}=s_{\nu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
. Multiplying these
two equalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{s}_{\mu}\overline{s}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\cdot s_{\nu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(s_{\mu}s_{\nu}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}s_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{eq.lrcoeff.def}))}\end{subarray}}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda\text{
has length }\leq n\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]\text{)}\end{subarray}}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda\text{
has length }>n\end{subarray}}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\underbrace{s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.alt.lrr.=0}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\underbrace{s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.alt.sbar})}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda\text{
has length }>n\end{subarray}}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}0}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}.

This proves Lemma
4.20
.

Lemma 4.22
.

The family
\left(\overline{s}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
of elements of
\mathcal{L}
is

\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent.

Proof 4.23

(Proof of Lemma
4.22
.)
.

Let us define a
strict snake
to
be an
n
-tuple
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of integers satisfying
\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{2}>\cdots>\alpha_{n}
. It is easy to see that the map

\displaystyle\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{\text{strict
snakes}\right\},

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto\lambda+\rho

(26)

is a bijection.

It is also easy to see that if
\alpha
and
\beta
are two strict snakes,
then

\left(\text{the coefficient of }x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\text{ in }a_{\alpha}\right)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta},

(27)

where
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
is the Kronecker delta of
\alpha
and
\beta

(that is, the integer
\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\alpha=\beta;\\
0,&\text{if }\alpha\neq\beta\end{cases}
).

Now, assume that
\left(u_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}\in\mathbf{k}^{\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
be a
family of scalars with the property that
\left(\text{all but finitely many
snakes }\lambda\text{ satisfy }u_{\lambda}=0\right)
and

\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}=0.

(28)

We shall show that
u_{\lambda}=0
for all snakes
\lambda
.

Indeed, fix a snake
\mu
. Then,
\mu+\rho
is a strict snake (since the map
(
26
) is a bijection). Let us denote this
strict snake by
\beta
. Thus,
\beta=\mu+\rho
.

If
\lambda
is any snake, then
\lambda+\rho
is a strict snake (since the
map (
26
) is a bijection), and thus satisfies

\displaystyle\left(\text{the coefficient of }x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\text{ in }a_{\lambda+\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda+\rho,\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.alt.linind.coeff-kron}), applied to }\alpha=\lambda+\rho\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda+\rho,\mu+\rho}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}\beta=\mu+\rho\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}

(29)

(since
\lambda+\rho=\mu+\rho
holds if and only if
\lambda=\mu
holds).

From (
28
), we obtain

0=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}\underbrace{\overline{s}_{\lambda}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\overline{s}_{\lambda}\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=\dfrac{1}{a_{\rho}}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}a_{\lambda+\rho}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
a_{\rho}
, we obtain

0=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}a_{\lambda+\rho}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\left(\text{the coefficient of }x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\text{ in }0\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the coefficient of }x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\text{ in }\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}a_{\lambda+\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}\underbrace{\left(\text{the coefficient of }x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\text{ in }a_{\lambda+\rho}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.alt.linind.5}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}\delta_{\lambda,\mu}=u_{\mu},

so that

u_{\mu}=\left(\text{the coefficient of }x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\text{ in }0\right)=0.

Now, forget that we fixed
\mu
. We thus have proved that
u_{\mu}=0
for all
snakes
\mu
. In other words,
u_{\lambda}=0
for all snakes
\lambda
.

Forget that we fixed
\left(u_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
. We thus have shown that if
\left(u_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}\in\mathbf{k}^{\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
is a family of scalars with the property that

\left(\text{all but finitely many snakes }\lambda\text{ satisfy
}u_{\lambda}=0\right)
and
\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}u_{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}=0
, then
u_{\lambda}=0
for all snakes

\lambda
. In other words, the family
\left(\overline{s}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
of elements of
\mathcal{L}
is

\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent. This proves Lemma
4.22
.

Lemma
4.22
is actually part of a stronger claim: The family

\left(\overline{s}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module of symmetric Laurent
polynomials in
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
. We shall not need this, however, so
we omit the proof
7
7

7

Just in case: It follows easily from Lemma

4.18
and
[
GriRei20
, Remark 2.3.9(d)]
.
.

Recall Definition
4.2

(d)
. Our next lemma connects the
Laurent polynomials
\overline{s}_{\lambda}
and
\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}
for every snake
\lambda
; it is folklore (see
[
GriRei20
, Exercise
2.9.15(d)]
for an equivalent version), but we shall prove it for the
sake of completeness.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
\lambda
be a snake. Then,

\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right).

Here, of course,
\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)
means the result of substituting
x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}
for
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
in the
Laurent polynomial
\overline{s}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{L}
.

Proof 4.25

(Proof of Lemma
4.24
.)
.

Let
w_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be the
permutation of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
that sends each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
to
n+1-i
. The map
\mathfrak{S}_{n}\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{n},\ w\mapsto w\circ w_{0}
is a bijection (since
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is a group).

For each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we have

\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}=\lambda_{i}+\underbrace{\rho_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=n-i\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.snakes.f.rhoi=}))}\end{subarray}}=\lambda_{i}+n-i.

(30)

Proposition
4.4

(b)
shows that
\lambda^{\vee}

is a snake. Thus, using Proposition
4.4

(a)
, we
conclude that
\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)
is a snake. Let us denote
this snake by
\mu
. Thus,
\mu=\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)
. Hence,

\mu+\rho=\left(\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)\right)+\rho=\left(\lambda^{\vee}+\rho\right)+\left(1-n\right)
(this follows from
Proposition
4.4

(c)
, applied to
\lambda^{\vee}
,
\rho
and
1-n
instead of
\lambda
,
\mu
and
d
). Therefore,

a_{\mu+\rho}=a_{\left(\lambda^{\vee}+\rho\right)+\left(1-n\right)}=x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\lambda^{\vee}+\rho}

(31)

(by Lemma
4.11
, applied to
\lambda^{\vee}+\rho
and
1-n

instead of
\lambda
and
d
).

The definition of
\lambda^{\vee}
yields
\lambda^{\vee}=\left(-\lambda_{n},-\lambda_{n-1},\ldots,-\lambda_{1}\right)
. Hence, for each

i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we have

\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)_{i}=-\lambda_{n+1-i}.

(32)

Thus, for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mu_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)\right)_{i}\\
\text{(since }\mu=\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\rho_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=n-i\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.snakes.f.rhoi=}))}\end{subarray}}=\underbrace{\left(\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)\right)_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)_{i}+\left(1-n\right)\\
\text{(by the definition of
}\lambda^{\vee}+\left(1-n\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}+n-i

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=-\lambda_{n+1-i}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.alt.inverses.check}))}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\left(1-n\right)+n-i}_{=1-i}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i.

(33)

Hence, the definition of
a_{\mu+\rho}
yields

\displaystyle a_{\mu+\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\underbrace{x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{n}}}_{=\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(i\right)}^{\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}\underbrace{x_{w\left(i\right)}^{\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{i}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i}\\
\text{(since (\ref{pf.lem.alt.inverses.chec2}) yields }\left(\mu+\rho\right)_{i}=-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i}.

(34)

The definition of
a_{\lambda+\rho}
yields

\displaystyle a_{\lambda+\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\underbrace{x_{w\left(1\right)}^{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{1}}x_{w\left(2\right)}^{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{2}}\cdots x_{w\left(n\right)}^{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{n}}}_{=\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(i\right)}^{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}\underbrace{x_{w\left(i\right)}^{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{w\left(i\right)}^{\lambda_{i}+n-i}\\
\text{(since (\ref{pf.lem.alt.inverses.rho2}) yields }\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}=\lambda_{i}+n-i\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(i\right)}^{\lambda_{i}+n-i}.

Substituting
x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}
for
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
on both sides of this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{\lambda+\rho}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}\underbrace{\left(x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-1}\right)^{\lambda_{i}+n-i}}_{=x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-\left(\lambda_{i}+n-i\right)}}=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-\left(\lambda_{i}+n-i\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{w\circ w_{0}}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{w}\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}\underbrace{x_{\left(w\circ w_{0}\right)\left(i\right)}^{-\left(\lambda_{i}+n-i\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{w\left(n+1-i\right)}^{-\lambda_{i}-n+i}\\
\text{(since }-\left(\lambda_{i}+n-i\right)=-\lambda_{i}-n+i\\
\text{and }\left(w\circ w_{0}\right)\left(i\right)=w\left(w_{0}\left(i\right)\right)=w\left(n+1-i\right)\\
\text{(because }w_{0}\left(i\right)=n+1-i\text{))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }w\circ w_{0}\text{ for }w\text{ in the
sum,}\\
\text{since the map }\mathfrak{S}_{n}\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{n},\ w\mapsto w\circ w_{0}\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(n+1-i\right)}^{-\lambda_{i}-n+i}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\underbrace{\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(n+1-i\right)}^{-\lambda_{i}-n+i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(n+1-\left(n+1-i\right)\right)}^{-\lambda_{n+1-i}-n+\left(n+1-i\right)}\\
\text{(here, we have
substituted }n+1-i\text{ for }i\\
\text{in the product)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}\underbrace{x_{w\left(n+1-\left(n+1-i\right)\right)}^{-\lambda_{n+1-i}-n+\left(n+1-i\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i}\\
\text{(since
}-\lambda_{n+1-i}-n+\left(n+1-i\right)=-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i\\
\text{and
}n+1-\left(n+1-i\right)=i\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{w}\prod\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}}x_{w\left(i\right)}^{-\lambda_{n+1-i}+1-i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=a_{\mu+\rho}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.alt.inverses.ampr}))}\end{subarray}}=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}\underbrace{a_{\mu+\rho}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\lambda^{\vee}+\rho}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.alt.inverses.0}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\lambda^{\vee}+\rho}.

(37)

On the other hand, let us denote the snake
\left(\underbrace{0,0,\ldots,0}_{n\text{ times}}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by
\varnothing
; note that it
satisfies
\varnothing^{\vee}=\left(\underbrace{-0,-0,\ldots,-0}_{n\text{
times}}\right)=\left(\underbrace{0,0,\ldots,0}_{n\text{ times}}\right)=\varnothing
. We have proved (
37
) for any snake

\lambda
; thus, we can apply (
37
) to
\varnothing

instead of
\lambda
. We thus obtain

a_{\varnothing+\rho}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\varnothing^{\vee}+\rho}.

In view of
\varnothing+\rho=\rho
and
\underbrace{\varnothing^{\vee}}_{=\varnothing}+\rho=\varnothing+\rho=\rho
, we can rewrite this as

a_{\rho}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\rho}.

(38)

The definition of
\overline{s}_{\lambda}
yields
\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}
. Hence,
a_{\lambda+\rho}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}a_{\rho}
. Substituting
x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}

for
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
on both sides of this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{\lambda+\rho}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{s}_{\lambda}a_{\rho}\right)\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)\cdot\underbrace{a_{\rho}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\rho}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.alt.inverses.a2}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\rho}.

Comparing this with (
37
), we find

\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\rho}=\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}a_{\lambda^{\vee}+\rho}.

(39)

The element
\left(-1\right)^{w_{0}}x_{\Pi}^{1-n}
of
\mathcal{L}
is
invertible (since
x_{\Pi}
is invertible), and thus we can cancel it from the
equality (
39
). As a result, we obtain

\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)\cdot a_{\rho}=a_{\lambda^{\vee}+\rho}.

(40)

But the definition of
\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}
yields

\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}=\dfrac{a_{\lambda^{\vee}+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

(by (
40
)). This proves Lemma
4.24
.

4.2
h_{k}^{+}
,
h_{k}^{-}
and the Pieri rule

Definition 4.26
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, we define two Laurent polynomials

h_{k}^{+}\in\mathcal{L}
and
h_{k}^{-}\in\mathcal{L}
by

\displaystyle h_{k}^{+}

\displaystyle=h_{k}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle h_{k}^{-}

\displaystyle=h_{k}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right).

Note that if
k\in\mathbb{Z}
is negative, then
h_{k}^{+}=\underbrace{h_{k}}_{=0}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=0
and
h_{k}^{-}=0
(similarly).

We begin by describing
h_{k}^{+}
as a Schur Laurent polynomial:

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the partition
\left(k\right)
is a nonnegative snake (when regarded as the
n
-tuple
\left(k,0,0,\ldots,0\right)
), and satisfies

\overline{s}_{\left(k\right)}=h_{k}^{+}.

Proof 4.28

(Proof of Lemma
4.27
.)
.

The partition
\left(k\right)
has length

\leq n
(since it has length
\leq 1
, but we have
n\geq 1
). Thus, it is a
nonnegative snake (since every partition having length
\leq n
is a
nonnegative snake) and belongs to
\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.
Hence, Lemma
4.16
(applied to
\lambda=\left(k\right)
)
yields
\overline{s}_{\left(k\right)}=s_{\left(k\right)}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=h_{k}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
(since
s_{\left(k\right)}=h_{k}
). Comparing this with

h_{k}^{+}=h_{k}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
, we obtain

\overline{s}_{\left(k\right)}=h_{k}^{+}
. This proves Lemma

4.27
.

Next, we need to know what happens when a Schur Laurent polynomial

\overline{s}_{\lambda}
is multiplied by some
h_{k}^{+}
. The answer to this
question is classically given by the
first Pieri rule
; we shall state
it in a form that will be most convenient to us. To do so, we introduce some
more notation:

Definition 4.29
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Then, we define the

size

\left|\lambda\right|
of
\lambda
to be the integer

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}
.

Definition 4.30
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Then, we write that

\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
if and only if we have

\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}\geq\lambda_{n}.

(41)

In other words, we write that
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
if and only if we
have

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{i}\geq\lambda_{i}\text{ for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{i}\geq\mu_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}\right).

The following properties of the sizes of
n
-tuples are obvious:

Proposition 4.31
.

(a)

If
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, then
\left|\lambda+\mu\right|=\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\mu\right|
.

(b)

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

\left|\lambda+d\right|=\left|\lambda\right|+nd
.

(c)

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, then
\left|\lambda^{\vee}\right|=-\left|\lambda\right|
.

The relation
\rightharpoonup
defined in Definition
4.30
has the
following simple properties:

Proposition 4.32
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

(a)

If
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
, then both
\lambda
and

\mu
are snakes.

(b)

We have
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
if and only if

\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee}
.

(c)

Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, we have
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
if and only if
\mu+d\rightharpoonup\lambda+d
.

Proof 4.33

(Proof of Proposition
4.32
.)
.

(a)
Assume that

\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
. Thus, the chain of inequalities
(
41
) holds (by Definition
4.30
). But this
chain of inequalities implies both
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}

and
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
. Thus,
\mu
and

\lambda
are snakes. This proves Proposition
4.32

(a)
.

(b)
The definition of
\mu^{\vee}
yields
\mu^{\vee}=\left(-\mu_{n},-\mu_{n-1},\ldots,-\mu_{1}\right)
. Similarly,
\lambda^{\vee}=\left(-\lambda_{n},-\lambda_{n-1},\ldots,-\lambda_{1}\right)
. Hence, we
have
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee}
if and only if we have

-\lambda_{n}\geq-\mu_{n}\geq-\lambda_{n-1}\geq-\mu_{n-1}\geq\cdots\geq-\lambda_{1}\geq-\mu_{1}

(because of Definition
4.30
).

Thus, we have the following chain of equivalences:

\displaystyle\left(\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee}\right)\

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(-\lambda_{n}\geq-\mu_{n}\geq-\lambda_{n-1}\geq-\mu_{n-1}\geq\cdots\geq-\lambda_{1}\geq-\mu_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\lambda_{n}\leq\mu_{n}\leq\lambda_{n-1}\leq\mu_{n-1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}\geq\lambda_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Definition \ref{def.horstr}}\right).

In other words, we have
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
if and only if

\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee}
. This proves Proposition

4.32

(b)
.

(c)
This follows easily from Definition
4.30
.

We can now state the Pieri rule in the form we need:

Proposition 4.34
.

Let
\lambda
be a snake. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

(42)

This can be proven directly using alternants; but let us give a proof based on
known theory:

Proof 4.35

(Proof of Proposition
4.34
.)
.

We follow the same right-shift
strategy as we did in our proof of Lemma
4.13
. Thus, our proof
shall consist of two steps:

Step 1:
We will prove Proposition
4.34
in the
particular case when
\lambda
is nonnegative.

Step 2:
We will use Lemma
4.18
to derive the general
case of Proposition
4.34
from this particular case.

Here are the details of the two steps:

Step 1:
Let us prove that Proposition
4.34
holds in
the particular case when
\lambda
is nonnegative.

Indeed, let us assume that
\lambda
is nonnegative. We must prove the
equality (
42
).

If
k<0
, then both sides of this equality are
0

8
8

8

Proof.
Assume that
k<0
. We must show that both
sides of (
42
) are
0
.
Indeed, from
k<0
, we obtain
h_{k}=0
, thus
h_{k}^{+}=\underbrace{h_{k}}_{=0}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=0
. Hence,
\underbrace{h_{k}^{+}}_{=0}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}=0
. In other words, the left hand side
of (
42
) is
0
.
It remains to show that the right hand side of (
42
)
is
0
. This will follow if we can show that the sum on this right hand side
is empty, i.e., that there exists no snake
\mu
such that
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
and
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k
. So
let us show this.
Let
\mu
be a snake such that
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
and
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k
. We shall derive a
contradiction.
From
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
, we obtain
\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}\geq\lambda_{n}
(by the definition of
“
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
”). Hence,

\mu_{i}\geq\lambda_{i}
for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. Thus,

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\mu_{i}}_{\geq\lambda_{i}}\geq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}
. In other words,
\left|\mu\right|\geq\left|\lambda\right|
(since
\left|\mu\right|=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{n}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}
and similarly
\left|\lambda\right|=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}
). Hence,
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|\geq 0
. But this contradicts

\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k<0
.
Forget that we fixed
\mu
. We thus have found a contradiction whenever
\mu

is a snake such that
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda
and
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k
. Hence, there exists no such
snake
\mu
. In other words, the sum on the right hand side of
(
42
) is empty. Hence, the right hand side of
(
42
) is
0
. This completes our proof.
. Thus, the
equality (
42
) holds if
k<0
. Therefore, for the rest
of Step 1, we WLOG assume that
k\geq 0
. In other words,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that
\lambda
is a partition of length
\leq n
(since
\lambda
is a
nonnegative snake). In other words,
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.

We will use some standard notations concerning partitions. Specifically:

•

If
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right)
and

\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\ldots\right)
are two
partitions, then we will write
\alpha\subseteq\beta
if and only if each

i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
satisfies
\alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}
.
(This is precisely the definition of
\alpha\subseteq\beta
given in

[
GriRei20
, Definition 2.3.1]
.)

•

If
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right)
and

\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\ldots\right)
are two
partitions, then we say that
\alpha/\beta

is a horizontal strip
if
they satisfy

\beta\subseteq\alpha\text{ and }\left(\text{every }i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}\text{ satisfies }\beta_{i}\geq\alpha_{i+1}\right).

(This is not literally how a “horizontal
strip” is defined in
[
GriRei20
]
, but it is equivalent to
that definition; the equivalence follows from
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.7.5(a)]
.)

•

If
\alpha
and
\beta
are two partitions, and if
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
then we say that
\alpha/\beta

is a horizontal
k
-strip
if

\alpha/\beta
is a horizontal strip and we have
\left|\alpha\right|-\left|\beta\right|=k
. (This is equivalent to the definition of a
“horizontal
k
-strip” in
[
GriRei20
, §2.7]
).

We note the following claim:

Claim 1:
We have

\displaystyle\left\{\text{partitions }\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]\text{ such that }\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\text{snakes }\mu\text{ such that }\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda\text{ and}\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\right\}.

[
Proof of Claim 1:
This is an exercise in unraveling definitions and
recalling that partitions in
\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
are the
same as nonnegative snakes. We leave the details to the reader (who can also
find them expanded in the detailed version
[
Grinbe20
]
of this paper).]

From the first Pieri rule (
[
GriRei20
, (2.7.1)]
9
9

9

This also appears
in
[
MenRem15
, Theorem 5.3]
, in
[
Stanle01
, Theorem 7.15.7]
and in

[
Egge19
, Theorem 9.3]
.
, applied to
k
instead of
n
), we obtain

s_{\lambda}h_{k}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda^{+}\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda^{+}/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}s_{\lambda^{+}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a
horizontal }k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}

(here, we have renamed the summation index
\lambda^{+}
as
\mu
).

Evaluating both sides of this equality at
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
, we find

\displaystyle\left(s_{\lambda}h_{k}\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a
horizontal }k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a
horizontal }k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length }\leq n\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length
}>n\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.alt.lrr.=0}),}\\
\text{applied to }\mu\text{ instead of }\lambda\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a
horizontal }k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length }\leq n\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length }>n\end{subarray}}0}_{=0}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a
horizontal }k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length }\leq n\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right).

In view of

\displaystyle\left(s_{\lambda}h_{k}\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.alt.sbar})}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{h_{k}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{k}^{+}\\
\text{(since }h_{k}^{+}=h_{k}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\cdot h_{k}^{+}=h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda},

we can rewrite this as

h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length
}\leq n\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right).

(43)

But we have the following equality of summation signs:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal
}k\text{-strip;}\\
\mu\text{ has length }\leq n\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\mu\text{ has length }\leq n\text{;}\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right];\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal
}k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the partitions }\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\text{ that have length
}\leq n\\
\text{are precisely the partitions }\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]\end{array}\right).

Thus, we can rewrite (
43
) as

\displaystyle h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right];\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal
}k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{s}_{\mu}\\
\text{(since Lemma \ref{lem.alt.sbar}}\\
\text{(applied to }\mu\text{ instead of }\lambda\text{)}\\
\text{yields
}\overline{s}_{\mu}=s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right];\\
\mu/\lambda\text{ is a horizontal }k\text{-strip}\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(by Claim 1)}\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

This proves (
42
). Thus, Proposition

4.34
is proved under the assumption that
\lambda
is
nonnegative. This completes Step 1.

Step 2:
Let us now prove Proposition
4.34
in the
general case.

The snake
\lambda
may or may not be nonnegative. However, there exists some
integer
d
such that the snake
\lambda+d
is nonnegative
10
10

10

Indeed,
this can be proved in the same way as it was proved during Step 2 of the proof
of Lemma
4.13
above.
. Consider this
d
.

The map
\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\text{snakes}\right\},\ \mu\mapsto\mu+d
is a bijection. (Indeed, its inverse is the map

\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\text{snakes}\right\},\ \mu\mapsto\mu-d
.) Moreover, every snake
\mu
satisfies

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left|\mu+d\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|\mu\right|+nd\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.size-snake.easies}
{(b)})}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{\left|\lambda+d\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|\lambda\right|+nd\\
\text{(by Proposition
\ref{prop.size-snake.easies} {(b)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\mu\right|+nd\right)-\left(\left|\lambda\right|+nd\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|.

(44)

For any snake
\mu
, we have the logical equivalence

\left(\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\mu+d\rightharpoonup\lambda+d\right)

(by Proposition
4.32

(c)
). In other words, for
any snake
\mu
, we have the logical equivalence

\left(\mu+d\rightharpoonup\lambda+d\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda\right).

Hence, we have the following equality of summation signs:

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu+d\rightharpoonup\lambda+d;\ \left|\mu+d\right|-\left|\lambda+d\right|=k\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu+d\right|-\left|\lambda+d\right|=k\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}

(45)

(by (
44
)).

The snake
\lambda+d
is nonnegative; thus, we can apply Proposition

4.34
to
\lambda+d
instead of
\lambda
(because in Step 1,
we have proved that Proposition
4.34
holds in the particular
case when
\lambda
is nonnegative). Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda+d}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda+d;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda+d\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu+d\rightharpoonup\lambda+d;\ \left|\mu+d\right|-\left|\lambda+d\right|=k\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.alt.pieri1.s2.sumeq}))}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\overline{s}_{\mu+d}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\mu}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.alt.smove},}\\
\text{applied to }\mu\text{ instead of }\lambda\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\mu+d\text{ for }\mu\text{ in the sum,}\\
\text{since the map }\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\text{snakes}\right\},\ \mu\mapsto\mu+d\\
\text{is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\mu}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

Comparing this with

h_{k}^{+}\cdot\underbrace{\overline{s}_{\lambda+d}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.alt.smove})}\end{subarray}}=h_{k}^{+}\cdot x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\lambda},

we obtain

h_{k}^{+}\cdot x_{\Pi}^{d}\overline{s}_{\lambda}=x_{\Pi}^{d}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

We can divide both sides of this equality by
x_{\Pi}^{d}
(since
x_{\Pi}^{d}\in\mathcal{L}
is invertible (because
x_{\Pi}\in\mathcal{L}
is
invertible)), and thus obtain

h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

This proves Proposition
4.34
. Thus, Step 2 is complete, and
Proposition
4.34
is proven.

Using Lemma
4.24
, we can “turn Proposition

4.34
upside down”, obtaining the following
analogous result for
h_{k}^{-}
instead of
h_{k}^{+}
:

Proposition 4.36
.

Let
\lambda
be a snake. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

h_{k}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\lambda\rightharpoonup\mu;\ \left|\lambda\right|-\left|\mu\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

(46)

Proof 4.37

(Proof of Proposition
4.36
.)
.

It is easy to see (from
Definition
4.2

(d)
) that
\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}=\lambda
. Likewise,
\left(\mu^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}=\mu
for any
snake
\mu
. Hence, the map
\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\text{snakes}\right\},\ \mu\mapsto\mu^{\vee}
(which is
well-defined because of Proposition
4.4

(b)
) is
inverse to itself. Thus, this map is a bijection.

If
\mu
is a snake, then

\text{we have }\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda\text{ if and only if }\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee}

(47)

(by Proposition
4.32

(b)
). Moreover, if
\mu
is
a snake, then

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left|\lambda^{\vee}\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=-\left|\lambda\right|\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.size-snake.easies}
{(c)})}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{\left|\mu^{\vee}\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=-\left|\mu\right|\\
\text{(by Proposition
\ref{prop.size-snake.easies} {(c)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(-\left|\lambda\right|\right)-\left(-\left|\mu\right|\right)=\left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|.

(48)

But comparing the definitions of
h_{k}^{-}
and
h_{k}^{+}
easily yields

h_{k}^{-}=h_{k}^{+}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right).

Also, Lemma
4.24
yields
\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}=\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)
. Multiplying these two equalities, we obtain

\displaystyle h_{k}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}

\displaystyle=h_{k}^{+}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(h_{k}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.alt.pieri1})}\end{subarray}}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\lambda;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee};\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.alt.pieri2.1}))}\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee};\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right).

Comparing this with

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu;\ \left|\lambda^{\vee}\right|-\left|\mu\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee};\ \left|\lambda^{\vee}\right|-\left|\mu^{\vee}\right|=k\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee};\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.alt.pieri2.2}))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\overline{s}_{\mu^{\vee}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{s}_{\mu}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.alt.inverses},}\\
\text{applied
to }\mu\text{ instead of }\lambda\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\mu^{\vee}\text{ for }\mu\text{ in the sum,
since}\\
\text{the map }\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\text{snakes}\right\},\ \mu\mapsto\mu^{\vee}\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu^{\vee};\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\lambda\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right),

we obtain

h_{k}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda^{\vee}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\lambda^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu;\ \left|\lambda^{\vee}\right|-\left|\mu\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

We have proved this equality for any snake
\lambda
. Thus, we can apply it to

\lambda^{\vee}
instead of
\lambda
(since Proposition

4.4

(b)
shows that
\lambda^{\vee}
is a snake).
We thus obtain

h_{k}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}\rightharpoonup\mu;\ \left|\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}\right|-\left|\mu\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

In view of
\left(\lambda^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}=\lambda
, this can be
rewritten as follows:

h_{k}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\lambda\rightharpoonup\mu;\ \left|\lambda\right|-\left|\mu\right|=k\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

This proves Proposition
4.36
.

4.3
Computing
\overline{s}_{\alpha}

Convention 4.2
.

From now on, for the rest of Section
4
, we assume that
n\geq 2
.

Our next goal is to obtain a simple formula for the Schur polynomial

\overline{s}_{\alpha}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
, where

\alpha
is as in Theorem
2.2
. The first step is the following definition:

Definition 4.38
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
b\ominus a
will denote the
snake
\left(b,0^{n-2},-a\right)
. (This is indeed a well-defined snake,
since
n\geq 2
and since
b\geq 0\geq-a
.)

Proposition 4.39
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}.

(49)

Proof 4.40

(Proof of Proposition
4.39
.)
.

We must prove the equality
(
49
). If (at least) one of the integers
a
and
b
is
negative, then this equality boils down to
0=0

11
11

11

Indeed, its
left hand side is
0
in this case because every negative integer
k

satisfies
h_{k}^{-}=0
and
h_{k}^{+}=0
; but its right hand side is also
0

in this case, because the negativity of
\min\left\{a,b\right\}
causes
the sum to become empty.
. Hence, for the rest of this proof, we WLOG assume
that none of the integers
a
and
b
is negative. Hence,
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that each
k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\min\left\{a,b\right\}\right\}

satisfies
b-k\in\mathbb{N}
(since
k\leq\min\left\{a,b\right\}\leq b
)
and
a-k\in\mathbb{N}
(likewise). Hence, the snakes
\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)
on the right hand side of the equality
(
49
) are well-defined.

Lemma
4.27
(applied to
k=b
) yields that the partition
\left(b\right)
is a nonnegative snake (when regarded as the
n
-tuple
\left(b,0,0,\ldots,0\right)
), and satisfies
\overline{s}_{\left(b\right)}=h_{b}^{+}
.

Now, Proposition
4.36
(applied to
\lambda=\left(b\right)
and
k=a
) yields

h_{a}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\left(b\right)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\left(b\right)\rightharpoonup\mu;\ \left|\left(b\right)\right|-\left|\mu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

In view of
\overline{s}_{\left(b\right)}=h_{b}^{+}
and
\left|\left(b\right)\right|=b
, we can rewrite this as

h_{a}^{-}\cdot h_{b}^{+}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\left(b\right)\rightharpoonup\mu;\ b-\left|\mu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}.

(50)

Now, we claim the following:

Claim 1:
The snakes
\mu
satisfying
\left(b\right)\rightharpoonup\mu
and
b-\left|\mu\right|=a
are precisely the
snakes of the form
\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)
for

k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\min\left\{a,b\right\}\right\}
.

[
Proof of Claim 1:
The proof of this claim is straightforward (and
can be found elaborated in the detailed version
[
Grinbe20
]
of this paper).]

Now, Claim 1 shows that

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\left(b\right)\rightharpoonup\mu;\ b-\left|\mu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\min\left\{a,b\right\}\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}

(indeed, it is clear that the snakes
\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)
for
k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\min\left\{a,b\right\}\right\}
are all distinct). Hence, (
50
) becomes

h_{a}^{-}\cdot h_{b}^{+}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\left(b\right)\rightharpoonup\mu;\ b-\left|\mu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\min\left\{a,b\right\}\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}.

This proves Proposition
4.39
.

Proposition 4.41
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

\overline{s}_{b\ominus a}=h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}.

(Recall that every negative integer
k
satisfies
h_{k}^{-}=0
and
h_{k}^{+}=0
.)

Proof 4.42

(Proof of Proposition
4.41
.)
.

We have
\min\left\{a,b\right\}\in\mathbb{N}
(since
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
), so that
\min\left\{a,b\right\}\geq 0
.

Proposition
4.39
yields

\displaystyle h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}=\underbrace{\overline{s}_{\left(b-0\right)\ominus\left(a-0\right)}}_{=\overline{s}_{b\ominus a}}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have split off the addend for }k=0\text{ from the sum,}\\
\text{since }\min\left\{a,b\right\}\geq 0\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{s}_{b\ominus a}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}.

(53)

Proposition
4.39
(applied to
a-1
and
b-1
instead of
a
and

b
) yields

\displaystyle h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\min\left\{a-1,b-1\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(\left(b-1\right)-k\right)\ominus\left(\left(a-1\right)-k\right)}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}-1}\overline{s}_{\left(\left(b-1\right)-k\right)\ominus\left(\left(a-1\right)-k\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\min\left\{a-1,b-1\right\}=\min\left\{a,b\right\}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(\left(b-1\right)-\left(k-1\right)\right)\ominus\left(\left(a-1\right)-\left(k-1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have substituted }k-1\text{ for
}k\text{ in the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\left(b-1\right)-\left(k-1\right)=b-k\text{ and }\left(a-1\right)-\left(k-1\right)=a-k\right).

Subtracting this equality from (
53
), we obtain

h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}=\left(\overline{s}_{b\ominus a}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}\right)-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\min\left\{a,b\right\}}\overline{s}_{\left(b-k\right)\ominus\left(a-k\right)}=\overline{s}_{b\ominus a}.

This proves Proposition
4.41
.

Corollary 4.43
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Define the partition

\alpha=\left(a+b,a^{n-2}\right)
. Then,
\alpha
is a nonnegative snake
and satisfies

\overline{s}_{\alpha}=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right).

Proof 4.44

(Proof of Corollary
4.43
.)
.

From
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, we
obtain
a+\underbrace{b}_{\geq 0}\geq a\geq 0
. Hence,
\alpha
is a partition
of length
\leq n
(since
n-1\leq n
). In other words,
\alpha
is a
nonnegative snake.

It is easy to see that
\alpha=\left(b\ominus a\right)+a
(since

\alpha=\left(a+b,a^{n-2}\right)=\left(a+b,\underbrace{a,a,\ldots,a}_{n-2\text{ times}},0\right)
and
b\ominus a=\left(b,0^{n-2},-a\right)=\left(b,\underbrace{0,0,\ldots,0}_{n-2\text{ times}},-a\right)
). Hence,

\displaystyle\overline{s}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\overline{s}_{\left(b\ominus a\right)+a}=x_{\Pi}^{a}\overline{s}_{b\ominus a}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
Lemma \ref{lem.alt.smove}, applied to }\lambda=b\ominus a\text{ and
}d=a\right)

\displaystyle=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Proposition \ref{prop.ominus.s}}\right).

This proves Corollary
4.43
.

We notice that the expression
h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}
in
Proposition
4.41
can be rewritten as
\det\left(\begin{array}[c]{cc}h_{a}^{-}&h_{a-1}^{-}\\
h_{b-1}^{+}&h_{b}^{+}\end{array}\right)
. This suggests a way to generalize Proposition
4.41

(as well as the first Jacobi–Trudi formula
[
GriRei20
, (2.4.16)]
). Such a
generalization indeed exists, and has been proved by Koike as well as by Hamel
and King; see Proposition
5.1
below.

4.4
The sets
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
and a formula for

h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\overline{s}_{\mu}

We shall next aim for a formula for
h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\overline{s}_{\mu}

(for a snake
\mu
and integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
), which will be obtained in
a straightforward way by applying Propositions
4.34
and

4.36
. We will need the following definition:

Definition 4.45
.

Let
\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then,
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
shall denote the set of all snakes

\nu
satisfying the four conditions

\mu\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b.

Lemma 4.46
.

Let
\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Assume that
\gamma
is not a snake. Then,
\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|=0
.

Proof 4.47

(Proof of Lemma
4.46
.)
.

Let
\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
. We shall obtain a contradiction.

Indeed, we have
\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
. In other words,

\nu
is a snake satisfying the four conditions

\mu\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b

(by the definition of
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
). Thus, in
particular, we have
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu
. Hence, Proposition

4.32

(a)
(applied to
\gamma
and
\nu
instead
of
\mu
and
\lambda
) yields that both
\nu
and
\gamma
are snakes. Hence,

\gamma
is a snake. This contradicts the fact that
\gamma
is not a snake.

Now, forget that we fixed
\nu
. We thus have obtained a contradiction for
each
\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
. Hence, there exists no

\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
. In other words, the set

R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
is empty. Thus,
\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|=0
. This proves Lemma

4.46
.

Lemma 4.48
.

Let
\mu
be a snake. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\overline{s}_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}.

Proof 4.49

(Proof of Lemma
4.48
.)
.

Proposition
4.36

(with the letters
\lambda
,
k
and
\mu
renamed as
\mu
,
a
and
\nu
)
says that

h_{a}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\nu}.

(54)

Proposition
4.34
(with the letters
\lambda
,
k
and
\mu

renamed as
\nu
,
b
and
\gamma
) says that

h_{b}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\nu}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\gamma}

(55)

for each snake
\nu
.

Now,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\overline{s}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=h_{b}^{+}\cdot\underbrace{h_{a}^{-}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\nu}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.Rmab.formula.1}))}\end{subarray}}=h_{b}^{+}\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \underbrace{h_{b}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\nu}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\gamma}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.Rmab.formula.2}))}\end{subarray}}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ is a
snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ is a snake;}\\
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\ \ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a;\\
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\end{subarray}}}\overline{s}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\ \ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{
is a snake;}\\
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a;\\
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu;\ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)}\\
\text{(by the definition of }R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\gamma}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\ \ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)}\overline{s}_{\gamma}}_{=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}.

This proves Lemma
4.48
.

Corollary 4.50
.

Let
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
. Let

a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Define the partition
\alpha=\left(a+b,a^{n-2}\right)
. Then, every
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
satisfies

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|.

(56)

Here, we understand
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}
to mean
0
if
\lambda
is not
a partition (i.e., if
\lambda
is not a nonnegative snake).

Proof 4.51

(Proof of Corollary
4.50
.)
.

Corollary
4.43
shows
that
\alpha
is a nonnegative snake and satisfies

\overline{s}_{\alpha}=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right).

(57)

Every snake
\gamma
satisfies

\overline{s}_{\gamma+a}=x_{\Pi}^{a}\overline{s}_{\gamma}

(58)

(by Lemma
4.18
, applied to
\gamma
and
a
instead of

\lambda
and
d
).

We have
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
(since
\alpha
is a
nonnegative snake). Hence, Lemma
4.20
(applied to
\alpha
and

\mu
instead of
\mu
and
\nu
) yields

\displaystyle\overline{s}_{\alpha}\overline{s}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda\text{ is nonnegative}\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(since the
partitions }\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]\text{
are}\\
\text{precisely the nonnegative snakes)}\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda\text{ is nonnegative}\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\text{ is a snake}}c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda\text{ is not
nonnegative}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since we understand }c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\\
\text{to
mean }0\text{ if }\lambda\text{ is not}\\
\text{a nonnegative snake)}\end{subarray}}\overline{s}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\text{ is a snake}}c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\text{ is a snake;}\\
\lambda\text{ is not nonnegative}\end{subarray}}0\overline{s}_{\lambda}}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\text{ is a snake}}c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\lambda\text{ is a snake}}c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{s}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\overline{s}_{\alpha}\overline{s}_{\mu}=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)\overline{s}_{\mu}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.cor.Rmab.lr.1})}\right)

\displaystyle=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\underbrace{h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\overline{s}_{\mu}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.Rmab.formula})}\end{subarray}}-x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\underbrace{h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\overline{s}_{\mu}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.Rmab.formula},}\\
\text{applied to }a-1\text{ and
}b-1\\
\text{instead of }a\text{ and }b\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}-x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\overline{s}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left(\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\right)\underbrace{x_{\Pi}^{a}\overline{s}_{\gamma}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{s}_{\gamma+a}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.cor.Rmab.lr.2}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\text{ is a snake}}\left(\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\right)\overline{s}_{\gamma+a}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\text{ is a snake}}\left(\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|\right)\underbrace{\overline{s}_{\left(\lambda-a\right)+a}}_{=\overline{s}_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\lambda-a\text{ for }\gamma\text{ in the
sum,}\\
\text{since the map }\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\text{snakes}\right\},\ \lambda\mapsto\lambda-a\\
\text{is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\text{ is a snake}}\left(\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|\right)\overline{s}_{\lambda}.

We can compare coefficients on both sides of this equality (since
Lemma
4.22
shows that the family
\left(\overline{s}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\left\{\text{snakes}\right\}}
of
elements of
\mathcal{L}
is
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent), and thus
conclude that

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for every snake }\lambda\text{.}

This proves (
56
) in the case when
\lambda
is a snake.

However, it is easy to see that (
56
) also holds in the
case when
\lambda
is not a snake
12
12

12

Proof.
Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be such that
\lambda
is not a snake. We must show that
(
56
) holds for this
\lambda
.
We have assumed that
\lambda
is not a snake. Hence,
\lambda-a
is not a
snake (because it is easy to see from Definition
4.2
that

\lambda
is a snake if and only if
\lambda-a
is a snake). Thus, Lemma

4.46
(applied to
\gamma=\lambda-a
) yields
\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|=0
. Also, Lemma

4.46
(applied to
\lambda-a
,
a-1
and
b-1
instead of
\gamma
,

a
and
b
) yields
\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|=0
. On the other hand,
\lambda
is not a snake, and thus not a
nonnegative snake. Hence,
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}=0
(since we have defined

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}
to be
0
if
\lambda
is not a nonnegative snake).
Comparing this with
\underbrace{\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|}_{=0}-\underbrace{\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|}_{=0}=0
, we obtain
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda}=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda-a\right)\right|
. In other words,
(
56
) holds.
Thus, we have shown that (
56
) holds in the case when

\lambda
is not a snake.
. Thus, (
56
) always holds. This
proves Corollary
4.50
.

4.5
The map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}

Convention 4.3
.

For the whole Subsection
4.5
, we shall use Convention

3.3
(not only for
n
-tuples
a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
, but for any

n
-tuples
a
). This convention does not conflict with Convention

4.1
, because both conventions define
\gamma_{i}
in the same
way when
\gamma
is an
n
-tuple and
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}

(whereas the latter convention does not define
\gamma_{i}
for any other
values of
i
).

Convention
3.3
does conflict with our old convention (from
Section
1
) to identify partitions with finite tuples: Indeed, if
we let
\gamma
be the
n
-tuple
\left(\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{n\text{
times}}\right)
, then Convention
3.3
yields
\gamma_{n+1}=\gamma_{1}=1
when we regard
\gamma
as an
n
-tuple, but we get

\gamma_{n+1}=0
if we regard
\gamma
as a partition. We shall resolve this
conflict by agreeing
not to identify partitions with finite tuples in
Subsection
4.5
. (Thus, in particular, we will not identify a
nonnegative snake
\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with its corresponding partition
\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n},0,0,0,\ldots\right)\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
.)

Let us now apply the results of Section
3
