. The abelian group

\left(\mathbb{Z},+,0\right)
of integers is totally ordered (in the usual
way). Thus, Example
3.3
(applied to
\left(\mathbb{A},\ast,e\right)=\left(\mathbb{Z},+,0\right)
) shows that there
is a semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
(that is, a semifield
with ground set
\mathbb{Z}
, addition
\min
, multiplication
+
and unity

0
), called the
min tropical semifield
of
\left(\mathbb{Z},+,0\right)
. We have the following little dictionary between various
operations on this semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
and
familiar operations on integers:

•

The addition operation of the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the binary operation
\min
on
\mathbb{Z}
. That is,
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sum
a+b
understood with respect to the
semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is precisely the integer

\min\left\{a,b\right\}
.

•

Thus, (nonempty) finite sums in the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
are minima of finite sets of integers. That is, if

r\in\mathbb{N}
, and if
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
are any
r+1
integers,
then the sum
\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r}a_{k}
understood with respect to the semifield

\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is
\min\left\{a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\right\}=\min\left\{a_{k}\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,r\right\}\right\}
.

•

Furthermore, the multiplication operation of the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the addition
+
of integers. That is, for
any
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, the product
ab
understood with respect to the
semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is precisely the sum
a+b

understood with respect to the integer ring
\mathbb{Z}
. Meanwhile, the unity
of the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the integer
0
.

•

Thus, the division operation of the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the subtraction
-
of integers. That is, for any

a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, the quotient
\dfrac{a}{b}
understood with respect to the
semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is precisely the difference

a-b
understood with respect to the integer ring
\mathbb{Z}
.

•

For the same reason, squaring an element of the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is tantamount to doubling it as an integer. That
is, for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, the square
a^{2}
understood with respect to
the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the product
2a

understood with respect to the integer ring
\mathbb{Z}
.

•

For the same reason, taking reciprocals in the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is tantamount to negation of integers. That is,
for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, the reciprocal
\dfrac{1}{a}
understood with
respect to the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the integer

-a
understood with respect to the integer ring
\mathbb{Z}
.

•

For the same reason, finite products in the semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
are sums of integers. That is, if
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and if
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{r}
are any
r
integers, then the
product
\prod\limits_{k=1}^{r}a_{k}
understood with respect to the semifield

\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
is the sum
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r}a_{k}

understood with respect to the integer ring
\mathbb{Z}
.

Thus, applying Definition
3.5
to
\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
(and renaming everything
13
13

13

Namely, we are

•

renaming the (fixed)
n
-tuple
u
as
\mu
;

•

renaming the (variable)
n
-tuple
x
as
\gamma
(in order to avoid a
clash with the variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
);

•

renaming the elements
t_{r,j}
as
\tau_{r,j}
;

•

renaming the
n
-tuple
y
as
\eta
.

), we obtain the following:

Definition 4.52
.

Fix any
n
-tuple
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

We define a map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as follows:

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and

r\in\mathbb{N}
, define an element
\tau_{r,j}\in\mathbb{Z}
by

\displaystyle\tau_{r,j}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\underbrace{\gamma_{j+1}+\gamma_{j+2}+\cdots+\gamma_{j+k}}_{=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{j+i}}+\underbrace{\mu_{j+k+1}+\mu_{j+k+2}+\cdots+\mu_{j+r}}_{=\sum\limits_{i=k+1}^{r}\mu_{j+i}}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,r\right\}\right\}.

Define
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by setting

\eta_{i}=\mu_{i}+\left(\mu_{i-1}+\tau_{n-1,i-1}\right)-\left(\gamma_{i+1}+\tau_{n-1,i+1}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Set
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)=\eta
.

Applying Theorem
3.8
to
\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
(and renaming everything
14
14

14

Namely, we are

•

renaming the (fixed)
n
-tuple
u
as
\mu
;

•

renaming the (variable)
n
-tuple
x
as
\gamma
;

•

renaming the
n
-tuple
y
as
\eta
.

, and using our above dictionary), we thus obtain the following:

Theorem 4.53
.

Fix any
n
-tuple
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

(a)

The map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
is an involution (i.e., we have

\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mu}=\operatorname*{id}
).

(b)

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

be such that
\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
. Then,

\left(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\cdots+\eta_{n}\right)+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{n}\right)=2\left(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{n}\right).

(c)

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

be such that
\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
. Then,

\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{-\mu_{i+1},-\gamma_{i+1}\right\}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{-\mu_{i+1},-\eta_{i+1}\right\}

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(d)

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

be such that
\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
. Then,

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\gamma_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\mu_{i}\right).

We obtain the following corollaries from Theorem
4.53
:

Corollary 4.54
.

Fix any
n
-tuple
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Let

\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be such that

\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
. Then:

(a)

We have
\left|\eta\right|-\left|\mu\right|=\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|
.

(b)

We have

\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}=\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}

for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.

(c)

We have

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mu_{i}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}.

(d)

We have
\gamma=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\eta\right)
.

Proof 4.55

(Proof of Corollary
4.54
.)
.

(a)
Theorem

4.53

(b)
yields

\left(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\cdots+\eta_{n}\right)+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{n}\right)=2\left(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{n}\right).

In view of the equalities

\displaystyle\left|\eta\right|

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\cdots+\eta_{n},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\gamma\right|=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle\text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{n},

we can rewrite this as
\left|\eta\right|+\left|\gamma\right|=2\left|\mu\right|
. Equivalently,
\left|\eta\right|-\left|\mu\right|=\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|
. This proves Corollary
4.54

(a)
.

(b)
Let
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
. Then, Theorem

4.53

(c)
yields

\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{-\mu_{i+1},-\gamma_{i+1}\right\}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{-\mu_{i+1},-\eta_{i+1}\right\}.

In view of
\min\left\{-\mu_{i+1},-\gamma_{i+1}\right\}=-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}
and
\min\left\{-\mu_{i+1},-\eta_{i+1}\right\}=-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}
, we can rewrite
this as

\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\left(-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}\right)=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\left(-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}\right).

In other words,

\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}.

Equivalently,

\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}=\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}.

This proves Corollary
4.54

(b)
.

(c)
Theorem
4.53

(d)
yields

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\gamma_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\mu_{i}\right).

(59)

Now, we have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\left(\mu_{i}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\right)}_{=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\mu_{i}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\mu_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\mu_{i}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\gamma_{i}\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.cor.thm.fZ.full2.c.1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\gamma_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\gamma_{i}\right)\right)}_{=\gamma_{i}}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}.

This proves Corollary
4.54

(c)
.

(d)
Theorem
4.53

(a)
shows that
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mu}=\operatorname*{id}
. But recall that

\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
. Applying the map

\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
to both sides of this equality, we obtain

\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\eta\right)=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)\right)=\underbrace{\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\right)}_{=\operatorname*{id}}\left(\gamma\right)=\gamma.

This proves Corollary
4.54

(d)
.

We are now ready to prove the key lemma:

Lemma 4.56
.

Fix any
n
-tuple
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|.

Proof 4.57

(Proof of Lemma
4.56
.)
.

Define
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by

\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
. We must then prove that

\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|
.

We know that
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
is the set of all snakes

\nu
satisfying the four conditions

\mu\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b.

Likewise,
R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
is the set of all snakes
\nu

satisfying the four conditions

\mu\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \eta\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\eta\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a.

Now, fix
\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
. Thus,
\nu
is a snake
satisfying the four conditions

\mu\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma\rightharpoonup\nu\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b

(by the definition of
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
). In particular, we
have
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu
. In other words, we have

\mu_{1}\geq\nu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\nu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}\geq\nu_{n}

(60)

(by the definition of “
\mu\rightharpoonup\nu
”). Likewise, from
\gamma\rightharpoonup\nu
, we obtain

\gamma_{1}\geq\nu_{1}\geq\gamma_{2}\geq\nu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\gamma_{n}\geq\nu_{n}.

(61)

We define an
n
-tuple
\zeta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by setting

\zeta_{i}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

We shall prove that
\zeta\in R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
.

We begin by proving several auxiliary claims:

Claim 1:
We have
\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}\geq\zeta_{i}
for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
.

[
Proof of Claim 1:
Let
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. From
(
60
), we obtain
\mu_{i}\geq\nu_{i}
. From
(
61
), we obtain
\gamma_{i}\geq\nu_{i}
. Combining
\mu_{i}\geq\nu_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}\geq\nu_{i}
, we obtain
\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\geq\nu_{i}
. Now, the definition of
\zeta
yields

\zeta_{i}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\underbrace{\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}}_{\geq\nu_{i}}+\nu_{i}\leq\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\nu_{i}+\nu_{i}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}.

In other words,
\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}\geq\zeta_{i}
. This
proves Claim 1.]

Claim 2:
We have
\zeta_{i}\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}
for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.

[
Proof of Claim 2:
Let
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
. From
(
60
), we obtain
\nu_{i}\geq\mu_{i+1}
. From
(
61
), we obtain
\nu_{i}\geq\gamma_{i+1}
. Combining

\nu_{i}\geq\mu_{i+1}
and
\nu_{i}\geq\gamma_{i+1}
, we obtain
\nu_{i}\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}
. Now, the definition of

\zeta
yields

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}\\
\text{(by Corollary \ref{cor.thm.fZ.full2} {(b)})}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\nu_{i}}_{\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}}

\displaystyle\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}+\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}=\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}.

This proves Claim 2.]

Claim 3:
The
n
-tuple
\zeta
is a snake and satisfies

\mu\rightharpoonup\zeta
and
\eta\rightharpoonup\zeta
.

[
Proof of Claim 3:
For each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we
have
\mu_{i}\geq\zeta_{i}
(since
\mu_{i}\geq\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}\geq\zeta_{i}
(by Claim 1)). For each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
, we have
\zeta_{i}\geq\mu_{i+1}
(since Claim 2
yields
\zeta_{i}\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}\geq\mu_{i+1}
). Combining the preceding two sentences, we obtain

\mu_{1}\geq\zeta_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\zeta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}\geq\zeta_{n}.

In other words,
\mu\rightharpoonup\zeta
(by the definition of
“
\mu\rightharpoonup\zeta
”).

For each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we have
\eta_{i}\geq\zeta_{i}
(since
\eta_{i}\geq\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}\geq\zeta_{i}
(by Claim 1)). For each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
, we have

\zeta_{i}\geq\eta_{i+1}
(since Claim 2 yields
\zeta_{i}\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}\geq\eta_{i+1}
). Combining the preceding two
sentences, we obtain

\eta_{1}\geq\zeta_{1}\geq\eta_{2}\geq\zeta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\eta_{n}\geq\zeta_{n}.

In other words,
\eta\rightharpoonup\zeta
(by the definition of
“
\eta\rightharpoonup\zeta
”). Hence,
Proposition
4.32

(a)
(applied to
\eta
and

\zeta
instead of
\mu
and
\lambda
) yields that both
\zeta
and
\eta

are snakes. Hence,
\zeta
is a snake. This completes the proof of Claim 3.]

Claim 4:
We have
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\zeta\right|=b
and
\left|\eta\right|-\left|\zeta\right|=a
.

[
Proof of Claim 4:
We have

\displaystyle\left|\mu\right|

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{n}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\left|\zeta\right|

\displaystyle=\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}+\cdots+\zeta_{n}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}.

Subtracting these two equalities from one another, we find

\displaystyle\left|\mu\right|-\left|\zeta\right|

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\zeta_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\zeta\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\left(\mu_{i}-\left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}\right)\right)}_{=\left(\mu_{i}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\right)-\nu_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\left(\mu_{i}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\right)-\nu_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mu_{i}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}\\
\text{(by Corollary
\ref{cor.thm.fZ.full2} {(c)})}\end{subarray}}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\nu_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}}_{=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{n}=\left|\gamma\right|}-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\nu_{i}}_{=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+\cdots+\nu_{n}=\left|\nu\right|}=\left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b.

Furthermore,

\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|=\underbrace{\left(\left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|\right)}_{=a}-\underbrace{\left(\left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|\right)}_{=b}=a-b

and

\left|\eta\right|-\left|\zeta\right|=\underbrace{\left(\left|\eta\right|-\left|\mu\right|\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|\\
\text{(by Corollary \ref{cor.thm.fZ.full2} {(a)})}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\left(\left|\mu\right|-\left|\zeta\right|\right)}_{=b}=\underbrace{\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|}_{=a-b}+b=\left(a-b\right)+b=a.

Thus, Claim 4 is proven.]

We have now shown (in Claim 3 and Claim 4) that
\zeta
is a snake satisfying
the four conditions

\mu\rightharpoonup\zeta\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\mu\right|-\left|\zeta\right|=b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \eta\rightharpoonup\zeta\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\eta\right|-\left|\zeta\right|=a.

In other words,
\zeta\in R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
(by the definition
of
R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
).

Forget that we fixed
\nu
. Thus, for each
\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
, we have constructed a
\zeta\in R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
. Let us denote this
\zeta
by
\widetilde{\nu}
. We thus have
defined a map

\displaystyle R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{\nu}.

Let us denote this map by
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma,a,b}
. Its definition shows that

\displaystyle\left(\underbrace{\mathbf{g}_{\gamma,a,b}\left(\nu\right)}_{=\widetilde{\nu}}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}_{i}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}

(62)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }\widetilde{\nu}\text{ was defined as the }n\text{-tuple }\zeta\text{, whose entries}\\
\text{are given by }\zeta_{i}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}\end{array}\right)

for each
\nu\in R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
and each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
.

However, from
\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
, we obtain

\gamma=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\eta\right)
(by Corollary

4.54

(d)
). The relation between
\gamma
and
\eta

is thus symmetric. Hence, in the same way as we defined a map
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma,a,b}:R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\rightarrow R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
, we can define a map
\mathbf{g}_{\eta,b,a}:R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)\rightarrow R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
(by repeating the
above construction of
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma,a,b}
with
b
,
a
,
\eta
and

\gamma
taking the roles of
a
,
b
,
\gamma
and
\eta
, respectively). The
resulting map
\mathbf{g}_{\eta,b,a}
satisfies

\left(\mathbf{g}_{\eta,b,a}\left(\nu\right)\right)_{i}=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}+\nu_{i}

(65)

for each
\nu\in R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
and each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. (Indeed, this can be proved just as we proved
(
62
), but with
b
,
a
,
\eta
and
\gamma
taking the
roles of
a
,
b
,
\gamma
and
\eta
.)

Now it is easy to see (using (
62
) and
(
65
)) that the two maps
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma,a,b}
and

\mathbf{g}_{\eta,b,a}
are mutually inverse. Hence, these two maps are
invertible, i.e., are bijections.

Thus, there exists a bijection from
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
to

R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
(namely,
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma,a,b}
). This
yields
\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)\right)\right|
(since

\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
). This proves Lemma

4.56
.

Having learned a lot about the map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
, let us now connect it
to the map
\varphi
defined in Theorem
2.2
. For this, we shall use
the following lemma:

Lemma 4.58
.

Fix any
n
-tuple
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be an
n
-tuple. For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

\displaystyle\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\underbrace{\nu_{j+1}+\nu_{j+2}+\cdots+\nu_{j+k}}_{=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\nu_{j+i}}+\underbrace{\mu_{j+k+1}+\mu_{j+k+2}+\cdots+\mu_{j+n-1}}_{=\sum\limits_{i=k+1}^{n-1}\mu_{j+i}}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}\right\}.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be such that

\eta_{i}=\mu_{i}+\left(\mu_{i-1}+\tau_{i-1}\right)-\left(\nu_{i+1}+\tau_{i+1}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Then,
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\nu\right)=\eta
.

Proof 4.59

(Proof of Lemma
4.58
.)
.

Lemma
4.58
is obtained when we
apply Lemma
3.11
to

\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
(and rename everything
15
15

15

Namely, we are

•

renaming the (fixed)
n
-tuple
u
as
\mu
;

•

renaming the
n
-tuple
x
as
\nu
;

•

renaming the elements
q_{j}
as
\tau_{j}
;

•

renaming the
n
-tuple
z
as
\eta
.

, and use our above dictionary again).

We can now connect the map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
with the map
\varphi
from
Theorem
2.2
:

Lemma 4.60
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix any
n
-tuple
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Define a map
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as in Theorem
2.2
. Then,

\varphi\left(\omega\right)=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)+b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Proof 4.61

(Proof of Lemma
4.60
.)
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Define an
n
-tuple
\nu=\left(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by

\nu_{i}=\omega_{i}-a\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Thus,
\nu=\omega-a
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
i\#
denote the unique element of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
congruent to
i
modulo
n
.

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

\displaystyle\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\left(\nu_{\left(j+1\right)\#}+\nu_{\left(j+2\right)\#}+\cdots+\nu_{\left(j+k\right)\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.+\left(\mu_{\left(j+k+1\right)\#}+\mu_{\left(j+k+2\right)\#}+\cdots+\mu_{\left(j+n-1\right)\#}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}\vphantom{\left(\nu_{\left(j+1\right)\#}\right)}\right\}.

(66)

Define an
n
-tuple
\eta=\left(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by setting

\eta_{i}=\mu_{i\#}+\left(\mu_{\left(i-1\right)\#}+\tau_{\left(i-1\right)\#}\right)-\left(\nu_{\left(i+1\right)\#}+\tau_{\left(i+1\right)\#}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

The definition of
\varphi
then yields

\varphi\left(\omega\right)=\left(\eta_{1}+b,\eta_{2}+b,\ldots,\eta_{n}+b\right)=\eta+b.

(67)

Our plan is now to show that
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\nu\right)=\eta
. We
shall achieve this by applying Lemma
4.58
; but in order to do so,
we need to show that the assumptions of Lemma
4.58
are satisfied.

We shall do this piece by piece. First, we make the following two claims,
which both follow from Convention
3.3
:

Claim 1:
We have
\nu_{p\#}=\nu_{p}
for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Claim 2:
We have
\mu_{p\#}=\mu_{p}
for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The next claim is an easy consequence of Claims 1 and 2:

Claim 3:
For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\underbrace{\nu_{j+1}+\nu_{j+2}+\cdots+\nu_{j+k}}_{=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\nu_{j+i}}+\underbrace{\mu_{j+k+1}+\mu_{j+k+2}+\cdots+\mu_{j+n-1}}_{=\sum\limits_{i=k+1}^{n-1}\mu_{j+i}}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\ \mid\ k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}\right\}.

The next claim is an easy fact from elementary number theory:

Claim 4:
We have
\left(p\#+q\right)\#=\left(p+q\right)\#

for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Using Claim 4, we easily obtain the following:

Claim 5:
We have
\tau_{p\#}=\tau_{p}
for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Now, let
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. Then, the definition of
\eta

yields

\displaystyle\eta_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mu_{i\#}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mu_{i}\\
\text{(by Claim 2)}\end{subarray}}+\left(\underbrace{\mu_{\left(i-1\right)\#}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mu_{i-1}\\
\text{(by Claim 2)}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\tau_{\left(i-1\right)\#}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\tau_{i-1}\\
\text{(by Claim 5)}\end{subarray}}\right)-\left(\underbrace{\nu_{\left(i+1\right)\#}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\nu_{i+1}\\
\text{(by
Claim 1)}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\tau_{\left(i+1\right)\#}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\tau_{i+1}\\
\text{(by Claim 5)}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{i}+\left(\mu_{i-1}+\tau_{i-1}\right)-\left(\nu_{i+1}+\tau_{i+1}\right).

Now, forget that we fixed
i
. We thus have proved that

\eta_{i}=\mu_{i}+\left(\mu_{i-1}+\tau_{i-1}\right)-\left(\nu_{i+1}+\tau_{i+1}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Combining this with Claim 3, we conclude that the assumptions of Lemma

4.58
are satisfied. Hence, Lemma
4.58
yields

\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\nu\right)=\eta
. In view of
\nu=\omega-a
, this
rewrites as
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)=\eta
. Hence,

\eta=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)
, so that
(
67
) becomes

\varphi\left(\omega\right)=\underbrace{\eta}_{=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)}+b=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)+b.

This proves Lemma
4.60
.

4.6
The finale

Now, let us again use the convention (from Section
1
) by which we
identify partitions with finite tuples (and therefore identify partitions in

\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
with nonnegative snakes). This is no
longer problematic, since we are not using Convention
3.3
any more.

Lemma 4.62
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Define the two partitions

\alpha=\left(a+b,a^{n-2}\right)
and
\beta=\left(a+b,b^{n-2}\right)
.

Fix any partition
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]
. Consider the
map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
defined in
Definition
4.52
.

Then, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we have

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda+a}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b}.

Here, we understand
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda+a}
to mean
0
if
\lambda+a
is
not a partition, and likewise we understand
c_{\beta,\mu}^{\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b}
to mean
0
if
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b
is not a partition.

Proof 4.63

(Proof of Lemma
4.62
.)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Corollary

4.50
(applied to
\lambda+a
instead of
\lambda
) yields

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda+a}

\displaystyle=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\left(\lambda+a\right)-a\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\left(\lambda+a\right)-a\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda\right)\right|

(68)

(since
\left(\lambda+a\right)-a=\lambda
). On the other hand,

\beta=\left(\underbrace{a+b}_{=b+a},b^{n-2}\right)=\left(b+a,b^{n-2}\right)
. Hence, Corollary
4.50
(applied to
b
,
a
,
\beta

and
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b
instead of
a
,
b
,
\alpha

and
\lambda
) yields

\displaystyle c_{\beta,\mu}^{\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b}

\displaystyle=\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b\right)-b\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,b-1,a-1}\left(\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b\right)-b\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)\right)\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda\right)\right|\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.fZ.lr},}\\
\text{applied to }\gamma=\lambda\text{)}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{\left|R_{\mu,b-1,a-1}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)\right)\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda\right)\right|\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.fZ.lr},}\\
\text{applied to }\lambda\text{, }a-1\text{ and }b-1\\
\text{instead of }\gamma\text{, }a\text{
and }b\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b\right)-b=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\lambda\right)\right|-\left|R_{\mu,a-1,b-1}\left(\lambda\right)\right|.

Comparing this with (
68
), we find
c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\lambda+a}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)+b}
. This proves Lemma
4.62
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2.2
:

Proof 4.64

(Proof of Theorem
2.2
.)
.

The map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
is an involution
(by Theorem
4.53

(a)
), thus a bijection.

Let
\mathbf{a}^{-}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be the map that
sends each
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
to
\omega-a
. This map
\mathbf{a}^{-}

is clearly a bijection.

Let
\mathbf{b}^{+}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be the map that
sends each
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
to
\omega+b
. This map
\mathbf{b}^{+}

is clearly a bijection.

From Lemma
4.60
, we can easily see that

\varphi=\mathbf{b}^{+}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\circ\mathbf{a}^{-}.

Recall that the maps
\mathbf{b}^{+}
,
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
and
\mathbf{a}^{-}

are bijections. Hence, their composition
\mathbf{b}^{+}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\circ\mathbf{a}^{-}
is a bijection as well. In other words,
\varphi
is a
bijection (since
\varphi=\mathbf{b}^{+}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\circ\mathbf{a}^{-}
). This proves Theorem
2.2

(a)
.

(b)
Let
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Then, Lemma
4.60

yields
\varphi\left(\omega\right)=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)+b
. Hence,
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)+b=\varphi\left(\omega\right)
. But Lemma
4.62
(applied to

\lambda=\omega-a
) yields

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\left(\omega-a\right)+a}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)+b}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\varphi\left(\omega\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\omega-a\right)+b=\varphi\left(\omega\right)\right).

In view of
\left(\omega-a\right)+a=\omega
, this rewrites as

c_{\alpha,\mu}^{\omega}=c_{\beta,\mu}^{\varphi\left(\omega\right)}
. This
proves Theorem
2.2

(b)
.

5
Final remarks

5.1
Aside: A Jacobi–Trudi formula for Schur
Laurent polynomials

As mentioned above, Proposition
4.41
has the following
generalization, which can be obtained from an identity of Koike

[
Koike89
, Proposition 2.8]
(via the map
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
from

[
Koike89
]
and the correspondence between Schur Laurent polynomials and
rational representations of
\operatorname{GL}\left(n\right)
), which has
later been extended by Hamel and King
[
HamKin11
]
(see
[
HamKin11
, (6) and
(10)]
for the connection):
16
16

16

We recall Definition

4.1
, Definition
4.2

(a)
, Definition

4.6
, Definition
4.15
and Definition
4.26
, as
well as the conventions made in Section
1
, for the notations used
in this proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
with
p+q\leq n
. Let
\mathbf{a}=\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{p}\right)
and
\mathbf{b}=\left(b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{q}\right)
be two partitions. Let
\mathbf{b}\ominus\mathbf{a}
denote the snake
\left(b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{q},0^{n-p-q},-a_{p},-a_{p-1},\ldots,-a_{1}\right)
. Let
M
be the
\left(p+q\right)\times\left(p+q\right)
-matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{cases}h_{a_{p-i+1}+i-j}^{-},&\text{if }i\leq p;\\
h_{b_{i-p}-i+j}^{+},&\text{if }i>p\end{cases}\right)_{i,j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,p+q\right\}}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccccccc}h_{a_{p}}^{-}&h_{a_{p}-1}^{-}&\cdots&h_{a_{p}-p+1}^{-}&h_{a_{p}-p}^{-}&h_{a_{p}-p-1}^{-}&\cdots&h_{a_{p}-p-q+1}^{-}\\
h_{a_{p-1}+1}^{-}&h_{a_{p-1}}^{-}&\cdots&h_{a_{p-1}-p+2}^{-}&h_{a_{p-1}-p+1}^{-}&h_{a_{p-1}-p}^{-}&\cdots&h_{a_{p-1}-p-q+2}^{-}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{a_{1}+p-1}^{-}&h_{a_{1}+p-2}^{-}&\cdots&h_{a_{1}}^{-}&h_{a_{1}-1}^{-}&h_{a_{1}-2}^{-}&\cdots&h_{a_{1}-q}^{-}\\
h_{b_{1}-p}^{+}&h_{b_{1}-p+1}^{+}&\cdots&h_{b_{1}-1}^{+}&h_{b_{1}}^{+}&h_{b_{1}+1}^{+}&\cdots&h_{b_{1}+q-1}^{+}\\
h_{b_{2}-p-1}^{+}&h_{b_{2}-p}^{+}&\cdots&h_{b_{2}-2}^{+}&h_{b_{2}-1}^{+}&h_{b_{2}}^{+}&\cdots&h_{b_{2}+q-2}^{+}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{b_{q}-p-q+1}^{+}&h_{b_{q}-p-q+2}^{+}&\cdots&h_{b_{q}-q}^{+}&h_{b_{q}-q+1}^{+}&h_{b_{q}-q+2}^{+}&\cdots&h_{b_{q}}^{+}\end{array}\right).

Then,

\overline{s}_{\mathbf{b}\ominus\mathbf{a}}=\det M.

Remark 5.2
.

The analogous generalization of the second Jacobi–Trudi formula
(
[
GriRei20
, (2.4.17)]
) can easily be proved (although we leave both stating
and proving it to the reader). What makes it easy is the (fairly obvious) fact
that the elementary symmetric functions
e_{k}
satisfy

e_{k}\left(x_{1}^{-1},x_{2}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\right)=x_{\Pi}^{-1}e_{n-k}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. (See
[
GriRei20
, Definition 2.2.1]
for the
definition of
e_{k}
.)

Proposition
5.1
generalizes Proposition
4.41
(which
corresponds to the particular case when
p=1
and
q=1
) as well as the first
Jacobi–Trudi formula
[
GriRei20
, (2.4.16)]
(which corresponds to the
particular case
p=0
).

We notice that what we called
\mathbf{b}\ominus\mathbf{a}
in Proposition

5.1
has been called
\left[\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}\right]
in

[
Stembr87
]
.

We thank Grigori Olshanski for informing us of the provenance of Proposition

5.1
.

5.2
Questions on
\mathbf{f}_{u}

We shall now pose several questions about the birational involution

\mathbf{f}_{u}
studied in Section
3
. Convention

3.1
, Convention
3.2
and Convention

3.3
will be used throughout Subsection
5.2
.

5.2.1
\mathbf{f}_{u}
as a composition?

Most of our questions are meant to attempt seeing the involution

\mathbf{f}_{u}
from different directions. The first one is inspired by what
is now known as the “
toggle approach
” to dynamical combinatorics (see, e.g.,
[
Roby15
]
), but
is really an application of the age-old “divide and
conquer” paradigm to complicated maps:

Question 5.3
.

Is there an equivalent definition of
\mathbf{f}_{u}
as a composition of
toggles? (A
toggle
here means a birational map
\mathbb{K}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}^{n}
that changes only one entry of the
n
-tuple.
An example for a birational map that can be defined as a composition of
toggles is
birational rowmotion
– see, e.g.,
[
EinPro13
]
.
Cluster mutations, as in the theory of cluster algebras, are another example
of toggles.)

Another set of questions concern the
uniqueness
of
\mathbf{f}_{u}
.
While we defined the map
\mathbf{f}_{u}
explicitly, all we have then used
are the properties listed in Theorem
3.8
. Thus, it is a natural
question to ask whether these properties characterize
\mathbf{f}_{u}

uniquely. A pointwise version of this question can be asked as well: Given

x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
satisfying some of the
equalities in parts
(b)
,
(c)
and
(d)
of Theorem

3.8
, does it follow that
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
?
(Keep in mind that
u
is fixed.)

Depending on which equalities we require, we may of course get different
answers. Let us first ask what happens if we require the equalities from
Theorem
3.8

(c)
only:

5.2.2
Characterizing
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
via the
cyclic equations

Question 5.4
.

Given
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}

satisfying

\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}\right)=\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right)

(69)

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Does it follow that
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
or
y=x
?

Note that the “or
y=x
” part is needed
here, since
y=x
is obviously a solution to the equations
(
69
).

The following example shows that the answer to Question
5.4

is “no” if
\mathbb{K}
is the min tropical
semifield
\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
of the totally ordered
abelian group
\mathbb{Z}
.

Example 5.5
.

Let
k,g\in\mathbb{N}
with
g\geq k
. Let

\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
and
n=3
and
u=\left(0,0,g\right)
and
x=\left(1,2,0\right)
. Set
y=\left(k+1,2,k\right)
(where the “
+
” sign in “
k+1
” stands for addition of integers, not addition in

\mathbb{K}
). Then, the equations (
69
) hold in

\mathbb{K}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. (Restated in terms of standard
operations on integers, this is saying that

\min\left\{u_{i},x_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{-u_{i+1},-x_{i+1}\right\}=\min\left\{u_{i},y_{i}\right\}+\min\left\{-u_{i+1},-y_{i+1}\right\}

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.) This is straightforward to verify, and shows that
for a given
x
there can be an arbitrarily high (finite) number of

y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
satisfying the equations (
69
) for
all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

It is not hard to show (see
[
Grinbe20
]
for details) that this number
cannot be infinite.

Example
5.5
has shown that the answer to Question

5.4
is “no” when

\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
. However, the answer to
Question
5.4
is “yes” if

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}_{+}
and, more generally, if the semifield
\mathbb{K}

embeds into an integral domain:

Proposition 5.6
.

Assume that there is an integral domain

\mathbb{L}
such that the semifield
\mathbb{K}
is a subsemifield of

\mathbb{L}
(in the sense that
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{L}
and that the
operations
+
and
\cdot
of
\mathbb{K}
are restrictions of those of

\mathbb{L}
, whereas the unity of
\mathbb{K}
is the unity of
\mathbb{L}
).
Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Then, the only
n
-tuples
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}

satisfying the equations (
69
) for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}

are
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
and
y=x
.

See the detailed version
[
Grinbe20
]
of this paper for a rough outline of
the proof of Proposition
5.6
.

5.2.3
Characterizing
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
via the
cyclic equations and the product equation

Another avatar of the uniqueness question is the following:

Question 5.7
.

Given
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}

satisfying both (
69
) for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and

y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}\cdot x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}.

(70)

Does it follow that
y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
?

The answer to this question is definitely “yes” when
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}_{+}
, by essentially the
same argument that was used in Remark
3.18
. Again,
however, the answer is “no” when

\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
. For example, if

\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
and
n=4
and
u=\left(2,1,1,0\right)
and
x=\left(1,1,1,1\right)
, then the two
n
-tuples

\left(1,1,1,1\right)
and
\left(2,2,0,0\right)
both can be taken as

y
in Question
5.7
, but clearly cannot both equal

\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
. (On the other hand, if

\mathbb{K}=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+,0\right)
and
n=3
, then
the answer is “yes” again; this can
be shown by an unenlightening yet not particularly arduous case analysis.)

An even stronger version of Question
5.7
holds when

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}_{+}
:

Proposition 5.8
.

Assume that
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}_{+}
. Let

x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Assume that
(
69
) holds for all
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
, and assume that (
70
) holds. Then,

y=\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
.

The proof of Proposition
5.8
is sketched in the
detailed version
[
Grinbe20
]
of this paper.

5.2.4
Understanding Lemma
3.9

Another question concerns Lemma
3.9
:

Question 5.9
.

What is the “real meaning” of some of the
more complicated parts of Lemma
3.9
? In particular, Lemma

3.9

(g)
reminds of the Plücker relation for minors
of a
2\times m
-matrix; can it be viewed that way? (Such a proof would not be
superior to the one given above, as it wouldn’t be subtraction-free and thus
wouldn’t work natively over arbitrary semifields. But it would shine more
light on the lemma.)

5.3
On the genesis of
\varphi
(and
\mathbf{f}_{u}
)

As we mentioned in the introduction to this paper, Pelletier and Ressayre did
not conjecture Theorem
2.2
in this exact form; instead, they
conjectured the existence of a mysterious bijection
\varphi
that satisfies
Theorem
2.2

(b)
. Our definition of
\varphi
appears

ex caelis oblatus
; while we have seen that our
\varphi
duly plays
its part, it is far from clear how we have found it in the first place. The
following few paragraphs are meant to demystify this process.

We were looking for a bijection
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
satisfying Theorem
2.2

(b)
. In other
words, we were looking for a way to match
17
17

17

“Matching” means “perfect
matching” here – i.e., every coefficient on either side
should get a unique partner.
the nonzero coefficients in the product

s_{\alpha}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
(when expanded in the basis
\left(s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\left[n\right]}
of the
\mathbf{k}
-module of
symmetric polynomials in
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
) with the nonzero
coefficients in the product
s_{\beta}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
in such a way that
matching coefficients are equal.

The first step towards this goal was the discovery of the formula
s_{\alpha}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)=x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)
: our Corollary

4.43
. We originally proved this formula combinatorially, by
analyzing the structure of semistandard tableaux of shape
\alpha
.
18
18

18

Each of the first
a
columns of such a tableau would have
the form
\left(1,2,\ldots,i-1,i+1,\ldots,n\right)
for some
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, and these numbers
i
would weakly increase as one
moves right.
The proof of Corollary
4.43
given above
(using the Pieri rule) was an afterthought.

Corollary
4.43
was a visible step in the right direction,
as it moved the problem from the world of Littlewood–Richardson coefficients
into the simpler world of Pieri rules. Indeed, instead of expanding

s_{\alpha}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
, we now only had to expand
x_{\Pi}^{a}\cdot\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
, which looked like an
expansion that the Pieri rule could help with (to be fully honest, we only
knew the Pieri rule for multiplying by
h_{k}^{+}
; but we soon would find one
for multiplying by
h_{k}^{-}
). The
x_{\Pi}^{a}
factor was clearly a mere
distraction, but in order to get rid of it, we had to extend our polynomial
ring to the ring
\mathcal{L}
of Laurent polynomials (since
\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
is, in general, not a polynomial). This
extension had already been done by Stembridge in
[
Stembr87
]
, and all we
had to do was rename “staircases” as
“snakes”, define Schur Laurent polynomials
(by generalizing the alternant formula for Schur polynomials in the most
obvious way), extend some basic properties of Schur polynomials to Schur
Laurent polynomials, and find the “upside-down” Pieri rule (Proposition
4.36
).
None of this was difficult; in particular, the “upside-down” Pieri rule followed easily from the usual Pieri
rule using Lemma
4.24
(which is our main device for turning
things “upside down”). The Schur polynomial

s_{\nu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)
was generalized to the
Schur Laurent polynomial
\overline{s}_{\nu}
.

Thus our problem was reduced to matching the nonzero coefficients in the
product
\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
with the nonzero coefficients in the product
\left(h_{b}^{-}h_{a}^{+}-h_{b-1}^{-}h_{a-1}^{+}\right)\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
.
The products could both be expressed using the Pieri rules, but the
differences were still a distraction. At this point, we made a fortunate
guess: We hoped it would suffice to match the nonzero coefficients in the
product
h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
with the nonzero
coefficients in the product
h_{b}^{-}h_{a}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
. More
precisely, we hoped to find such a matching that would not depend on
a
and

b
; then it would also provide a matching between the nonzero coefficients in
the product
h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
and the nonzero
coefficients in the product
h_{b-1}^{-}h_{a-1}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
,
and therefore by taking differences we would obtain a matching between the
nonzero coefficients in the product
\left(h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}-h_{a-1}^{-}h_{b-1}^{+}\right)\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
and the nonzero coefficients
in the product
\left(h_{b}^{-}h_{a}^{+}-h_{b-1}^{-}h_{a-1}^{+}\right)\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
.

Thus we needed to expand
h_{a}^{-}h_{b}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
. Using
the Pieri rules, this was straightforward – the answer is in Lemma

4.48
. Our problem was to connect this result with what we
would similarly obtain from expanding
h_{b}^{-}h_{a}^{+}\cdot\overline{s}_{\mu}
. In other words, we wanted to construct a bijection
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
that would satisfy

\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any }a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Fixing
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we thus were looking for an
n
-tuple
\eta

(our
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)
-to-be) that would satisfy

\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|
.

In the case when
b=0
(in which case this equality would be equivalent to
saying “
\eta\rightharpoonup\mu
if and only if
\mu\rightharpoonup\gamma
”), we found such an
\eta
directly,
by setting

\eta_{i}=\mu_{i}+\mu_{i+1}-\gamma_{i+1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each
}i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\},

where indices are cyclic modulo
n
(so that
\mu_{0}=\mu_{n}
and
\nu_{0}=\nu_{n}
). This formula surprised us with its cyclic symmetry (which was
not expected from the original problem, and which foreshadowed the usefulness
of Convention
3.3
, although we thought nothing of it at that
point). Nevertheless, the formula failed in various examples for
b>0
, and we
could not easily fix it.

We tried to be more systematic. It was easy to rewrite the definition of

R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
as

\displaystyle R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\ \mid\ \left(\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}\geq\nu_{i}\geq\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}\text{ for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{and }\left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a\text{ and
}\left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b\right\}.

Thus, the size of this set would depend only

•

on the differences
\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}
for
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}
(each of which differences would determine the
“breathing space” for the corresponding

\nu_{i}
),

•

on the difference
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|
(which would have to equal
a-b
in order for the two conditions

\left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a
and
\left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b
to be satisfiable simultaneously),

•

as well as on something else we could not quite pinpoint (in order for

\left|\mu\right|-\left|\nu\right|=a
and
\left|\gamma\right|-\left|\nu\right|=b
to actually hold, as opposed
to merely
\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|=a-b
).

Analogous observations held for
R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)
.
With Occam’s razor in hand, we suspected that
\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|
could best be achieved by requiring these differences to be the
same for
\left(\mu,a,b,\gamma\right)
as for
\left(\mu,b,a,\eta\right)
. Thus, in particular, we hoped to have

\displaystyle\min\left\{\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\gamma_{i+1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mu_{i},\eta_{i}\right\}-\max\left\{\mu_{i+1},\eta_{i+1}\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}

and

\left|\mu\right|-\left|\gamma\right|=\left|\eta\right|-\left|\mu\right|.

(Due to the “mystery ingredient”, this would
likely neither be necessary nor sufficient for
\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\eta\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|
, but it looked like the right tree to bark up.) This is a system
of equations that whose solution is neither unique nor straightforward.
However, the system was a beacon rather than a destination to us, so we merely
needed something like a good solution.

Systems of equations involving sums, differences, minima and maxima belong to

tropical geometry
– a discipline we were not expert in and could not
hope to master quickly. However, we were aware of a surprisingly successful
strategy for taming such systems:
detropicalization
. The mainstay of
this strategy is the observation (made above in Example

3.3
) that the binary operations
\min
,
\max
,
+

and
-
are the addition, the “harmonic
addition”
19
19

19

Harmonic addition
is a binary
operation defined on any semifield. It sends any pair
\left(a,b\right)

of elements of the semifield to
\dfrac{1}{\dfrac{1}{a}+\dfrac{1}{b}}=\dfrac{ab}{a+b}
.
, the multiplication and the division of a certain
semifield (the min tropical semifield of
\left(\mathbb{Z},+,0\right)
, or
of whatever totally ordered abelian group our numbers belong to). Thus, even
if we could not solve our system, we could generalize it to arbitrary
semifields by replacing
\min
,
\max
,
+
and
-
by addition,
“harmonic addition”, multiplication and
division, respectively. Thus our system would become

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{i}+\gamma_{i}\right)/\dfrac{1}{\dfrac{1}{\mu_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{\gamma_{i+1}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{i}+\eta_{i}\right)/\dfrac{1}{\dfrac{1}{\mu_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{\eta_{i+1}}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}

and

\dfrac{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{n}}{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{n}}=\dfrac{\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\cdots\eta_{n}}{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{n}}.

Renaming
\mu
,
\gamma
and
\eta
as
u
,
x
and
y
, and simplifying the
fractions somewhat, we rewrote this as

\displaystyle\left(u_{i}+x_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(u_{i}+y_{i}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{u_{i+1}}+\dfrac{1}{y_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}

and

y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{n}\cdot x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}=\left(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{n}\right)^{2}.

This new system was a system of polynomial equations (at least after clearing
denominators), so we did the obvious thing: We left it to the computer for
small values of
n
(specifically,
n=2
,
n=3
and
n=4
) and looked at the
results. For
n=3
, the computer (SageMath’s
solve
function, to be
precise) laid out the following two solutions:

•

Solution 1:

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{1}\left(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{u_{1}x_{2}u_{3}-x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}},

\displaystyle y_{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{-u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}}{x_{1}x_{3}},

\displaystyle y_{3}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{2}u_{3}\left(x_{1}x_{3}-u_{1}u_{3}\right)}{u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}u_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}u_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}.

•

Solution 2:

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{1}u_{3}\left(u_{1}u_{2}+x_{1}u_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}\right)}{x_{2}\left(u_{1}u_{3}+u_{1}x_{3}+x_{1}x_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle y_{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{1}u_{2}\left(u_{2}u_{3}+x_{2}u_{3}+x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{x_{3}\left(u_{1}u_{2}+x_{1}u_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}\right)},

\displaystyle y_{3}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{2}u_{3}\left(u_{1}u_{3}+u_{1}x_{3}+x_{1}x_{3}\right)}{x_{1}\left(u_{2}u_{3}+x_{2}u_{3}+x_{2}x_{3}\right)}.

The computer did not know that we were trying to work over a semifield (which
had no subtraction), but we did, so we immediately discarded Solution 1 as
useless due to the minus signs. The question was whether Solution 2 would be
of any use. The omens were favorable: There were no minus signs; the
(unexpected, but not unwelcome) cyclic symmetry reared its head again;
finally, the nontrivial factors (such as
u_{1}u_{2}+x_{1}u_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}
)
had a structure that appeared in the definition of the geometric crystal
R-matrix (see, e.g.,
[
Etingo03
, (5)]
or
[
NouYam02
, (4.19)]
) – a
known successful case of detropicalization.

Solution 2 turned out to be generalizable indeed. Proving that the general
formula indeed produced a solution to our system (parts
(b)
and

(c)
of Theorem
3.8
) was not completely trivial, but not
hard either. (The first few parts of Lemma
3.9
were discovered
along the way.) Thus we had a candidate for the map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
(and
thus for the map
\varphi
, which was obtained from
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
by
shifting by
a
and
b
, corresponding to the
x_{\Pi}^{a}
factor that we had dropped).

Why was this map
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
a bijection? Again, we believed that the
easiest way lay through the birational realm (i.e., we had to detropicalize).
Computer experiments suggested that
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
was not only a
bijection but actually an involution (part
(a)
of Theorem

3.8
). The first proof of this we found was the one sketched in
Remark
3.18
; the alternative, computational proof that
we gave first was found afterwards.

Having found our bijection
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}
, we had to retrace our steps.
Most of this was straightforward. The equality
\left|R_{\mu,b,a}\left(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\right)\right)\right|=\left|R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)\right|
still had to be proved, but
this turned out to be rather easy (part
(d)
of Theorem

3.8
was discovered along the way, as the missing ingredient from
our previous analysis of the size of
R_{\mu,a,b}\left(\gamma\right)
).
The way the proof was written up in the end was mostly decided by concerns of
readability rather than authenticity; we believe that, had we followed the
logic of its discovery in our writeup, we would have lost more in clarity than
would be gained in motivation. We placed the study of the birational map

\mathbf{f}_{u}
(Section
3
) in front due to its self-contained
nature and possible applicability to different problems; likewise, Section

4
begins with general properties of Schur Laurent polynomials and
slowly progresses towards more technical lemmas tailored for the proof of
Theorem
2.2
. We would not be too surprised if some unnecessary
detours were made along our way (Lemma
3.9
appears a
particularly likely place for such), for which we apologize in advance (any
simplifications are appreciated).

5.4
The birational
R
-matrix connection

In this section, we shall connect the map
\mathbf{f}_{u}
from our Definition

3.5
with the
birational R-matrix
\eta
defined in

[
LamPyl12
, §6]
and studied further (e.g.) in
[
CheLin20
]
.

We fix a positive integer
n
and a semifield
\mathbb{K}
. We shall use
Convention
3.1
and Convention
3.3
.
Let us recall the definition of the birational R-matrix
\eta
(no relation to
the
\eta
in Theorem
2.2
):

Definition 5.10
.

We define a map
\eta:\mathbb{K}^{n}\times\mathbb{K}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}^{n}\times\mathbb{K}^{n}
as follows:

Let
a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
b\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be two
n
-tuples. For any

i\in\mathbb{Z}
, define an element
\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)\in\mathbb{K}
by

\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)=\sum\limits_{j=i}^{i+n-1}\underbrace{b_{i+1}b_{i+2}\cdots b_{j}}_{=\prod\limits_{p=i+1}^{j}b_{p}}\cdot\underbrace{a_{j+1}a_{j+2}\cdots a_{i+n-1}}_{=\prod\limits_{p=j+1}^{i+n-1}a_{p}}.

Define
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
by
setting

a_{i}^{\prime}=\dfrac{a_{i-1}\kappa_{i-1}\left(a,b\right)}{\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}

and

b_{i}^{\prime}=\dfrac{b_{i+1}\kappa_{i+1}\left(a,b\right)}{\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Set
\eta\left(a,b\right)=\left(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\right)
.

The map
\eta
we just defined is known as a
birational R-matrix
;
related maps have previously appeared in the literature (
[
BraKaz00
, Lemma
8.6]
,
[
Yamada01
, Definition 2.1]
,
[
Etingo03
, Proposition
3.1]
). In particular, the map
R
from
[
Etingo03
, Proposition
3.1]
is equivalent to
\eta
(at least up to technical issues of
where it is defined
20
20

20

Namely: We have defined our map
\eta
as a
literal map
\mathbb{K}^{n}\times\mathbb{K}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}^{n}\times\mathbb{K}^{n}
for any semifield
\mathbb{K}
, whereas

[
Etingo03
, Proposition 3.1]
defines
R
as a birational map
\left(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\right)^{n}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\right)^{n}\dashrightarrow\left(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\right)^{n}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\right)^{n}
. Neither of these two settings generalizes
the other, but it is not hard to transfer identities from one to the other (as
long as they are
subtraction-free
, i.e., no minus signs appear in
them).
). Indeed, it is not hard to see that the map
\eta
from Definition

5.10
becomes the map
R
from
[
Etingo03
, Proposition 3.1]
if we
set
x_{i}=b_{i+1}
and
y_{i}=a_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}=b_{i}^{\prime}
and

y_{i}^{\prime}=a_{i+1}^{\prime}
(that is, if we define
x_{i},y_{i},x_{i}^{\prime},y_{i}^{\prime}
this way, then the equalities
[
Etingo03
, (8), (9)
and (10)]
are satisfied, so that we have
R\left(x,y\right)=\left(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right)
where
R
is as defined in

[
Etingo03
, Proposition 3.1]
). This birational R-matrix
R
has its
origins in the theory of geometric crystals and total positivity. A related
map is the transformation
\left(x,a\right)\mapsto\left(y,b\right)
in

[
NouYam02
, §2.2]
(see also
[
Zygour18
]
).

Now, we shall see that the map
\eta
is intimately related to our map

\mathbf{f}_{u}
(even though
\mathbf{f}_{u}
transforms a single
n
-tuple

x
into a single
n
-tuple
y
using the fixed
n
-tuple
u
, while
\eta

takes a pair of two
n
-tuples to another such pair). In order to state this
relation, we define some more notation:

Definition 5.11
.

If
a\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
b\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
are two

n
-tuples, then we define two new
n
-tuples
ab\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and

\dfrac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
by setting

\left(ab\right)_{i}=a_{i}b_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\dfrac{a}{b}\right)_{i}=\dfrac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

We can now express the map
\mathbf{f}_{u}
from Definition
3.5

through the map
\eta
from Definition
5.10
as follows:

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
and
x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
be two

n
-tuples. Let
\left(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\right)=\eta\left(u,x\right)
. Then,

\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=u\dfrac{a^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}}.

Proof 5.13

(Proof of Theorem
5.12
.)
.

Set
a=u
and
b=x
. We shall use the
notations
\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)
from Definition
5.10
and
the notations
t_{r,j}
and
y
from Definition
3.5
. Then,

\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
(by Definition
3.5
).

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=i}^{i+n-1}b_{i+1}b_{i+2}\cdots b_{j}\cdot a_{j+1}a_{j+2}\cdots a_{i+n-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
the definition of }\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=i}^{i+n-1}x_{i+1}x_{i+2}\cdots x_{j}\cdot u_{j+1}u_{j+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }a=u\text{ and }b=x\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i+1}x_{i+2}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have
substituted }i+k\text{ for }j\text{ in the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=t_{n-1,i}

(71)

(since the definition of
t_{n-1,i}
yields
t_{n-1,i}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{i+1}x_{i+2}\cdots x_{i+k}\cdot u_{i+k+1}u_{i+k+2}\cdots u_{i+n-1}
).

However,
\left(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\right)=\eta\left(u,x\right)=\eta\left(a,b\right)
(since
u=a
and
x=b
). Hence, Definition

5.10
yields that

a_{i}^{\prime}=\dfrac{a_{i-1}\kappa_{i-1}\left(a,b\right)}{\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}

and

b_{i}^{\prime}=\dfrac{b_{i+1}\kappa_{i+1}\left(a,b\right)}{\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

Hence, for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle a_{i}^{\prime}/b_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{a_{i-1}\kappa_{i-1}\left(a,b\right)}{\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)}/\dfrac{b_{i+1}\kappa_{i+1}\left(a,b\right)}{\kappa_{i}\left(a,b\right)}=\dfrac{a_{i-1}\kappa_{i-1}\left(a,b\right)}{b_{i+1}\kappa_{i+1}\left(a,b\right)}=\dfrac{a_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{b_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }\kappa_{i-1}\left(a,b\right)=t_{n-1,i-1}\text{ (by
(\ref{pf.thm.eta-f1.k=t}), applied to }i-1\text{ instead of }i\text{)}\\
\text{and }\kappa_{i+1}\left(a,b\right)=t_{n-1,i+1}\text{ (by
(\ref{pf.thm.eta-f1.k=t}), applied to }i+1\text{ instead of }i\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }a=u\text{ and }b=x\right)

\displaystyle=y_{i}/u_{i}

(74)

(since the definition of
y
yields
y_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\dfrac{u_{i-1}t_{n-1,i-1}}{x_{i+1}t_{n-1,i+1}}
). Now, for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(u\dfrac{a^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=u_{i}\dfrac{a_{i}^{\prime}}{b_{i}^{\prime}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
Definition \ref{def.n-tup-frac}}\right)

\displaystyle=u_{i}\cdot\underbrace{a_{i}^{\prime}/b_{i}^{\prime}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=y_{i}/u_{i}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.eta-f1.fracs}))}\end{subarray}}=u_{i}\cdot y_{i}/u_{i}=y_{i}.

In other words,
u\dfrac{a^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}}=y
. Comparing this with

\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=y
, we obtain
\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)=u\dfrac{a^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}}
. This proves Theorem

5.12
.

We finish by stating some “gauge-invariance” properties for
\mathbf{f}_{u}
and
\eta
:

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
g,u,x\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Then,
\mathbf{f}_{gu}\left(gx\right)=g\mathbf{f}_{u}\left(x\right)
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
g,a,b\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Let
\left(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\right)=\eta\left(a,b\right)
. Then,
\left(ga^{\prime},gb^{\prime}\right)=\eta\left(ga,gb\right)
.

Both of these propositions can be proved by fairly simple computations, which
are left to the reader.
